Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You are given a rectangle grid. That grid's size is n × m. Let's denote the coordinate system on the grid. So, each point on the grid will have coordinates — a pair of integers (x, y) (0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m).

Your task is to find a maximum sub-rectangle on the grid (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2) so that it contains the given point (x, y), and its length-width ratio is exactly (a, b). In other words the following conditions must hold: 0 ≤ x_1 ≤ x ≤ x_2 ≤ n, 0 ≤ y_1 ≤ y ≤ y_2 ≤ m, $\frac{x_{2} - x_{1}}{y_{2} - y_{1}} = \frac{a}{b}$.

The sides of this sub-rectangle should be parallel to the axes. And values x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2 should be integers. [Image] 

If there are multiple solutions, find the rectangle which is closest to (x, y). Here "closest" means the Euclid distance between (x, y) and the center of the rectangle is as small as possible. If there are still multiple solutions, find the lexicographically minimum one. Here "lexicographically minimum" means that we should consider the sub-rectangle as sequence of integers (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2), so we can choose the lexicographically minimum one.


-----Input-----

The first line contains six integers n, m, x, y, a, b (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 10^9, 0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m, 1 ≤ a ≤ n, 1 ≤ b ≤ m).


-----Output-----

Print four integers x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2, which represent the founded sub-rectangle whose left-bottom point is (x_1, y_1) and right-up point is (x_2, y_2).


-----Examples-----
Input
9 9 5 5 2 1

Output
1 3 9 7

Input
100 100 52 50 46 56

Output
17 8 86 92### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×m grid with coordinates (x,y) where 0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m
2. We need to find a sub-rectangle that:
   - Contains the point (x,y)
   - Has exactly ratio (a,b) between width and height (width/height = a/b)
   - Has integer coordinates
3. Among all valid solutions, we want:
   - Closest to point (x,y) by Euclidean distance to center
   - If tie, lexicographically smallest (x₁,y₁,x₂,y₂)

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Mathematical Analysis

Given the ratio requirement: (x₂ - x₁) / (y₂ - y₁) = a/b

Let width = w = x₂ - x₁, height = h = y₂ - y₁
Then w/h = a/b, which means w = (a/b) × h

Since w and h must be integers, h must be a multiple of b, and w must be a multiple of a.
So we can write: h = k×b, w = k×a for some positive integer k.

### Step 2: Constraints for Valid Rectangle

For a rectangle with width w and height h containing (x,y):
- x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₂ = x₁ + w
- y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₂ = y₁ + h

This gives us:
- x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₁ + w → x - w ≤ x₁ ≤ x
- y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₁ + h → y - h ≤ y₁ ≤ y

Also, we need:
- x₁ ≥ 0, x₂ = x₁ + w ≤ n → x₁ ≤ n - w
- y₁ ≥ 0, y₂ = y₁ + h ≤ m → y₁ ≤ m - h

### Step 3: Find Valid k Values

For each k ≥ 1:
- w = k×a, h = k×b
- We need: x - w ≤ x ≤ x + w and y - h ≤ y ≤ y + h
- Valid x₁ range: max(0, x - w) to min(x, n - w)
- Valid y₁ range: max(0, y - h) to min(y, m - h)

### Step 4: Search Strategy

We need to:
1. Try different values of k starting from 1
2. For each k, find if there are valid positions
3. Among all valid rectangles, choose the one closest to (x,y) by center distance
4. If tie, choose lexicographically smallest

### Step 5: Optimization

The key insight is that we can't iterate over all k values from 1 to infinity. Instead, we need to find the valid range of k.

The constraints are:
- w = k×a ≤ n (since x₁ ≥ 0 and x₂ = x₁ + w ≤ n)
- h = k×b ≤ m (since y₁ ≥ 0 and y₂ = y₁ + h ≤ m)
- k×a ≤ x (from x₁ ≤ x and x₁ ≥ 0)
- k×b ≤ y (from y₁ ≤ y and y₁ ≥ 0)

Actually, let me reconsider the constraints more carefully.

For x₁: max(0, x - w) ≤ x₁ ≤ min(x, n - w)
For this to be possible: x - w ≤ x (always true) and x ≥ 0, and x ≤ n - w

So we need: x ≤ n - w → x ≤ n - k×a → k×a ≤ n - x → k ≤ (n - x) / a

Similarly: y ≤ m - h → y ≤ m - k×b → k ≤ (m - y) / b

Also: w = k×a ≥ 0 (always true), h = k×b ≥ 0 (always true)

So k ≤ min((n - x) / a, (m - y) / b)

Also k ≥ 1.

Additionally, we need valid x₁ range: x - w ≤ x₁ ≤ x, and x₁ ≥ 0, x₁ ≤ n - w
So we need x - w ≤ x (true), x - w ≤ n - w (which gives x ≤ n, true), and x ≥ 0.

The main constraint for k is: k ≤ min((n - x) / a, (m - y) / b)

But we also need the rectangle to contain (x,y), so:
- x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₁ + w → x - w ≤ x₁ ≤ x
- y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₁ + h → y - h ≤ y₁ ≤ y

For x₁ range to be non-empty: x - w ≤ x (true) and x ≥ max(0, x - w)
For this to be possible, we need x ≥ x - w (true), and x - w ≤ x (true), and we also need x - w ≤ n - w → x ≤ n (true).

Actually, the constraint is that x ≥ max(0, x - w) and x ≤ min(x, n - w).

For non-empty x₁ range: we need x - w ≤ min(x, n - w)

This gives: x - w ≤ x (always true) and x - w ≤ n - w → x ≤ n (true).

The binding constraint is: x ≥ max(0, x - w) and x₁ ≤ n - w → x ≤ n - w → x ≤ n - w → w ≤ n - x → k×a ≤ n - x → k ≤ (n - x) / a

Similarly: k ≤ (m - y) / b

So valid k values: 1 ≤ k ≤ min((n - x) // a, (m - y) // b)

But we also need to ensure that x - w ≤ x (true) and x ≥ 0, and y - h ≤ y (true) and y ≥ 0.

### Step 6: Implementation

```python
n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())

# Find maximum valid k
max_k = min((n - x) // a, (m - y) // b)

best_rect = None
best_distance_sq = float('inf')

# Try each valid k
for k in range(1, max_k + 1):
    w = k * a
    h = k * b
    
    # Calculate valid ranges for x1 and y1
    # x1 must satisfy: max(0, x - w) <= x1 <= min(x, n - w)
    x1_min = max(0, x - w)
    x1_max = min(x, n - w)
    
    # y1 must satisfy: max(0, y - h) <= y1 <= min(y, m - h)
    y1_min = max(0, y - h)
    y1_max = min(y, m - h)
    
    # Check if valid ranges exist
    if x1_min <= x1_max and y1_min <= y1_max:
        # For each valid (x1, y1), we have a valid rectangle
        # We want to maximize the distance to center? No, minimize distance to (x,y)
        # But the center is ((x1+x2)/2, (y1+y2)/2) = ((x1+x1+w)/2, (y1+y1+h)/2)
        
        # The center is at (x1 + w/2, y1 + h/2)
        # Distance squared to (x,y): (x1 + w/2 - x)^2 + (y1 + h/2 - y)^2
        
        # Since we want to minimize distance, we should choose x1 and y1 that minimize this
        # For fixed k, we can find optimal x1 and y1
        
        # For distance minimization, we want x1 as close as possible to x - w/2
        # and y1 as close as possible to y - h/2
        
        x1_opt = x1_min + min(abs(x1_max - (x - w/2)), abs((x - w/2) - x1_min))
        # Actually, we need to find the value of x1 in [x1_min, x1_max] that minimizes |x1 + w/2 - x|
        
        # The optimal x1 is the one that makes x1 + w/2 as close to x as possible
        # So x1 + w/2 = x → x1 = x - w/2
        # So we take the closest integer to x - w/2 within [x1_min, x1_max]
        
        target_x1 = x - w / 2
        if target_x1 < x1_min:
            x1_opt = x1_min
        elif target_x1 > x1_max:
            x1_opt = x1_max
        else:
            x1_opt = int(round(target_x1))
            # Ensure it's in range
            if x1_opt < x1_min:
                x1_opt = x1_min
            elif x1_opt > x1_max:
                x1_opt = x1_max
        
        target_y1 = y - h / 2
        if target_y1 < y1_min:
            y1_opt = y1_min
        elif target_y1 > y1_max:
            y1_opt = y1_max
        else:
            y1_opt = int(round(target_y1))
            if y1_opt < y1_min:
                y1_opt = y1_min
            elif y1_opt > y1_max:
                y1_opt = y1_max
        
        # But we also need to check if we get a valid point
        # Actually, we want the one that minimizes distance
        # We'll just evaluate at the boundaries and pick the minimum
        
        # Since we want to minimize the distance, and we have constraints,
        # we can evaluate at the boundaries of the range
        
        dists = []
        
        # Try all boundary points for small ranges, or just endpoints
        # For simplicity, try x1_min, x1_max, and the closest to target
        x1_candidates = [x1_min, x1_max]
        if x1_min < x1_max:
            # Add a few candidates around the optimal
            candidate_x1 = target_x1
            if candidate_x1 >= x1_min and candidate_x1 <= x1_max:
                x1_candidates.append(int(round(candidate_x1)))
        
        y1_candidates = [y1_min, y1_max]
        if y1_min < y1_max:
            candidate_y1 = target_y1
            if candidate_y1 >= y1_min and candidate_y1 <= y1_max:
                y1_candidates.append(int(round(candidate_y1)))
        
        # Evaluate all candidates
        for x1 in x1_candidates:
            for y1 in y1_candidates:
                if x1 < x1_min or x1 > x1_max or y1 < y1_min or y1 > y1_max:
                    continue
                x2 = x1 + w
                y2 = y1 + h
                center_x = (x1 + x2) / 2
                center_y = (y1 + y2) / 2
                dist_sq = (center_x - x) ** 2 + (center_y - y) ** 2
                if dist_sq < best_distance_sq:
                    best_distance_sq = dist_sq
                    best_rect = (x1, y1, x2, y2)
                elif dist_sq == best_distance_sq:
                    # Lexicographically smaller
                    current = (x1, y1, x2, y2)
                    previous = best_rect
                    if current < previous:
                        best_rect = current
        
        # Also, if this k gives valid range, we might have better points
        # But let's be more systematic
        
        # Actually, let's just pick x1 and y1 that minimize distance
        # We need to find x1 in [x1_min, x1_max] that minimizes |x1 + w/2 - x|
        # The optimal x1 is ceil(x - w/2) or floor(x - w/2) if in range
        
        # Find optimal x1
        optimal_x1 = x1_min
        if x1_min <= x1_max:
            # Find the value that minimizes |x1 + w/2 - x|
            # This is when x1 + w/2 is closest to x
            # So x1 closest to x - w/2
            target = x - w / 2
            if target < x1_min:
                optimal_x1 = x1_min
            elif target > x1_max:
                optimal_x1 = x1_max
            else:
                # target is in the range
                optimal_x1 = int(round(target))
                if optimal_x1 < x1_min:
                    optimal_x1 = x1_min
                elif optimal_x1 > x1_max:
                    optimal_x1 = x1_max
        
        # Find optimal y1
        optimal_y1 = y1_min
        target = y - h / 2
        if target < y1_min:
            optimal_y1 = y1_min
        elif target > y1_max:
            optimal_y1 = y1_max
        else:
            optimal_y1 = int(round(target))
            if optimal_y1 < y1_min:
                optimal_y1 = y1_min
            elif optimal_y1 > y1_max:
                optimal_y1 = y1_max
        
        x2 = optimal_x1 + w
        y2 = optimal_y1 + h
        
        # Check if this is valid
        if x1_min <= optimal_x1 <= x1_max and y1_min <= optimal_y1 <= y1_max:
            center_x = (optimal_x1 + x2) / 2
            center_y = (optimal_y1 + y2) / 2
            dist_sq = (center_x - x) ** 2 + (center_y - y) ** 2
            
            if best_rect is None or dist_sq < best_distance_sq:
                best_distance_sq = dist_sq
                best_rect = (optimal_x1, optimal_y1, x2, y2)
            elif dist_sq == best_distance_sq:
                # Lexicographic comparison
                current = (optimal_x1, optimal_y1, x2, y2)
                if current < best_rect:
                    best_rect = current

if best_rect is None:
    # This shouldn't happen with the constraints
    best_rect = (0, 0, 0, 0)

x1, y1, x2, y2 = best_rect
print(x1, y1, x2, y2)
```

```python
n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())

# Find maximum valid k
max_k = min((n - x) // a, (m - y) // b)

best_rect = None
best_distance_sq = float('inf')

for k in range(1, max_k + 1):
    w = k * a
    h = k * b
    
    # Valid x1 range: must contain x, so x1 <= x <= x1 + w
    # and x1 >= 0, x1 <= n - w

    # and y1 >= 0, y1 <= m - h
    y1_min = max(0, y - h)

    if x1_min > x1_max or y1_min > y1_max:
        continue

            y1_opt = y1_max

    candidates = []

    candidates.append((x1_min, y1_min))
    candidates.append((x1_min, y1_max))
    candidates.append((x1_max, y1_min))

        y1_opt_int = int(round(target_y1))
        if y1_min <= y1_opt_int <= y1_max:

            candidates.append((x1_max, y1_opt_int))

        y1_opt_int = int(round(target_y1))

            candidates.append((x1_opt_int, y1_opt_int))
    
    # Evaluate all candidates
    for x1_test in [c[0] for c in candidates]:
        for y1_test in [c[1] for c in candidates]:
            if x1_min <= x1_test <= x1_max and y1_min <= y1_test <= y1_max:

                if best_rect is None or dist_sq < best_distance_sq:

                        best_rect = (x1_test, y1_test, x2, y2)

x1, y1, x2, y2 = best_rect
```

```python
n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())

max_k = min((n - x) // a, (m - y) // b)

best_rect = None
best_distance_sq = float('inf')

for k in range(1, max_k + 1):
    w = k * a
    h = k * b
    
    # Valid ranges
    x1_min = max(0, x - w)
    x1_max = min(x, n - w)
    y1_min = max(0, y - h)
    y1_max = min(y, m - h)
    
    if x1_min > x1_max or y1_min > y1_max:
        continue
    
    # Find optimal x1 and y1 to minimize distance to (x,y)
    # Center is at (x1 + w/2, y1 + h/2)
    # We want to minimize |x1 + w/2 - x| and |y1 + h/2 - y|
    
    # Optimal x1: closest to x - w/2
    target_x1 = x - w / 2
    x1_opt = x1_min
    if x1_min <= target_x1 <= x1_max:
        x1_opt = int(round(target_x1))
    
    # Ensure x1_opt is in range
    if x1_opt < x1_min:
        x1_opt = x1_min
    elif x1_opt > x1_max:
        x1_opt = x1_max
    
    # Optimal y1: closest to y - h/2
    target_y1 = y - h / 2
    y1_opt = y1_min
    if y1_min <= target_y1 <= y1_max:
        y1_opt = int(round(target_y1))
    
    # Ensure y1_opt is in range
    if y1_opt < y1_min:
        y1_opt = y1_min
    elif y1_opt > y1_max:
        y1_opt = y1_max
    
    x2 = x1_opt + w
    y2 = y1_opt + h
    
    # Calculate distance to center
    center_x = (x1_opt + x2) / 2
    center_y = (y1_opt + y2) / 2
    dist_sq = (center_x - x) ** 2 + (center_y - y) ** 2
    
    if best_rect is None or dist_sq < best_distance_sq:
        best_distance_sq = dist_sq
        best_rect = (x1_opt, y1_opt, x2, y2)
    elif dist_sq == best_distance_sq:
        # Lexicographically smaller
        if (x1_opt, y1_opt, x2, y2) < best_rect:
            best_rect = (x1_opt, y1_opt, x2, y2)

x1, y1, x2, y2 = best_rect
print(x1, y1, x2, y2)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You are given a rectangle grid. That grid's size is n × m. Let's denote the coordinate system on the grid. So, each point on the grid will have coordinates — a pair of integers (x, y) (0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m).

Your task is to find a maximum sub-rectangle on the grid (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2) so that it contains the given point (x, y), and its length-width ratio is exactly (a, b). In other words the following conditions must hold: 0 ≤ x_1 ≤ x ≤ x_2 ≤ n, 0 ≤ y_1 ≤ y ≤ y_2 ≤ m, $\frac{x_{2} - x_{1}}{y_{2} - y_{1}} = \frac{a}{b}$.

The sides of this sub-rectangle should be parallel to the axes. And values x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2 should be integers. [Image] 

If there are multiple solutions, find the rectangle which is closest to (x, y). Here "closest" means the Euclid distance between (x, y) and the center of the rectangle is as small as possible. If there are still multiple solutions, find the lexicographically minimum one. Here "lexicographically minimum" means that we should consider the sub-rectangle as sequence of integers (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2), so we can choose the lexicographically minimum one.


-----Input-----

-----Output-----

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×m grid with coordinates (x,y) where 0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m
2. We need to find a sub-rectangle that:
   - Contains the given point (x,y)
   - Has length-width ratio exactly a:b (where length = x₂-x₁, width = y₂-y₁)
   - Is as close as possible to the point (x,y) in Euclidean distance
   - If multiple solutions, choose lexicographically smallest

## Key Insights

1. The ratio condition: (x₂-x₁)/(y₂-y₁) = a/b
2. This means: b(x₂-x₁) = a(y₂-y₁)
3. We need to find integer values for x₁, y₁, x₂, y₂ such that:
   - 0 ≤ x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₂ ≤ n
   - 0 ≤ y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₂ ≤ m
   - b(x₂-x₁) = a(y₂-y₁)

## Mathematical Analysis

Let's define:
- width = x₂ - x₁
- height = y₂ - y₁

We need: width/height = a/b, so width = (a/b) × height

Since width and height must be integers, height must be a multiple of b, and width must be a multiple of a.

Let height = k×b, then width = k×a for some positive integer k.

So we have:
- x₂ - x₁ = k×a
- y₂ - y₁ = k×b

With constraints:
- x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₁ + k×a ≤ n
- y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₁ + k×b ≤ m

## Finding Valid Values of k

For a given k:
- x₁ ≤ x ≤ x + k×a
- x₁ + k×a ≤ n → x₁ ≤ n - k×a
- y₁ ≤ y ≤ y + k×b  
- y₁ + k×b ≤ m → y₁ ≤ m - k×b

So we need:
- x₁ ≥ max(0, x - k×a)
- x₁ ≤ min(x, n - k×a)
- y₁ ≥ max(0, y - k×b)
- y₁ ≤ min(y, m - k×b)

For valid k, we need:
- max(0, x - k×a) ≤ min(x, n - k×a)
- max(0, y - k×b) ≤ min(y, m - k×b)

## Solving for k

We need to find the largest possible k such that all constraints are satisfied, but we also need to consider distance to the center.

The center of the rectangle is at ((x₁+x₂)/2, (y₁+y₂)/2) = ((x₁ + x₁ + k×a)/2, ((y₁ + y₁ + k×b)/2) = (x₁ + k×a/2, y₁ + k×b/2)

Distance squared = (x₁ + k×a/2 - x)² + (y₁ + k×b/2 - y)²

To minimize distance, we want x₁ and y₁ as close as possible to x and y respectively.

For a fixed k, we want to:
1. Choose x₁ to minimize |x₁ - (x - k×a/2)|, but x₁ must satisfy constraints
2. Choose y₁ to minimize |y₁ - (y - k×b/2)|, but y₁ must satisfy constraints

## Algorithm Steps

1. Find all possible values of k (from 1 to maximum)
2. For each k, calculate valid ranges for x₁ and y₁
3. Find the optimal (x₁, y₁) for each k that minimizes distance
4. Among all valid k values, pick the one with minimum distance (and lexicographic order as tiebreaker)

## Implementation

```python
import math

def solve():
    n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Find maximum possible k
    max_k = min(n // a, m // b)
    
    best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = None, None, None, None
    best_dist_squared = float('inf')
    
    # Try all possible k values
    for k in range(1, max_k + 1):
        # Width and height for this k
        width = k * a
        height = k * b
        
        # Constraints for x1 and x2
        # x1 <= x <= x1 + width <= n
        # So: x1 >= max(0, x - width)
        #     x1 <= min(x, n - width)
        
        x1_min = max(0, x - width)
        x1_max = min(x, n - width)
        
        # Constraints for y1 and y2
        # y1 <= y <= y1 + height <= m
        # So: y1 >= max(0, y - height)
        #     y1 <= min(y, m - height)
        
        y1_min = max(0, y - height)
        y1_max = min(y, m - height)
        
        # Check if valid range exists
        if x1_min <= x1_max and y1_min <= y1_max:
            # For minimum distance, we want x1 and y1 as close as possible to x and y
            
            # Optimal x1: should be as close as possible to x
            # But must satisfy constraints
            x1_opt = x1_max  # Try the largest possible x1 (closest to x)
            if x1_opt < x:
                x1_opt = x1_min  # Try the smallest possible
            
            # Actually, we need to be more careful about distance
            # The distance is minimized when we get (x1, y1) closest to (x, y)
            
            # Find x1 that minimizes distance to center
            # Center x-coordinate: x1 + width/2
            # We want x1 + width/2 as close as possible to x
            # So x1 should be as close as possible to x - width/2
            
            center_x = x - width / 2
            x1_candidate = max(x1_min, min(x1_max, int(center_x)))
            
            # Check if we can get closer with boundary values
            dist_x1 = float('inf')
            x1_candidates = []
            
            # Consider the boundary values
            if x1_min <= x1_max:
                x1_candidates.append(x1_min)
                x1_candidates.append(x1_max)
                # Also try values around center_x if they're in range
                if x1_min <= center_x <= x1_max:
                    x1_candidates.append(int(center_x))
                    if int(center_x) - 1 >= x1_min:
                        x1_candidates.append(int(center_x) - 1)
                    if int(center_x) + 1 <= x1_max:
                        x1_candidates.append(int(center_x) + 1)
            
            # Find best x1 for this k
            best_x1_for_k = x1_min
            best_dist_for_k = float('inf')
            
            for cand_x1 in x1_candidates:
                if not (x1_min <= cand_x1 <= x1_max):
                    continue
                    
                # Find best y1 for this x1
                center_y = y - height / 2
                y1_candidate = max(y1_min, min(y1_max, int(center_y)))
                
                y1_candidates = []
                if y1_min <= y1_max:
                    y1_candidates.append(y1_min)
                    y1_candidates.append(y1_max)
                    if y1_min <= center_y <= y1_max:
                        y1_candidates.append(int(center_y))
                        if int(center_y) - 1 >= y1_min:
                            y1_candidates.append(int(center_y) - 1)
                        if int(center_y) + 1 <= y1_max:
                            y1_candidates.append(int(center_y) + 1)
                
                for cand_y1 in y1_candidates:
                    if not (y1_min <= cand_y1 <= y1_max):
                        continue
                    
                    x2 = cand_x1 + width
                    y2 = cand_y1 + height
                    
                    # Check if valid
                    if x2 <= n and y2 <= m:
                        # Calculate distance squared to center of rectangle
                        center_rect_x = (cand_x1 + x2) / 2
                        center_rect_y = (cand_y1 + y2) / 2
                        dist_sq = (center_rect_x - x) ** 2 + (center_rect_y - y) ** 2
                        
                        if dist_sq < best_dist_for_k:
                            best_x1_for_k = cand_x1
                            best_y1_for_k = cand_y1
                            best_dist_for_k = dist_sq
                        elif dist_sq == best_dist_for_k:
                            # Lexicographic comparison: (x1, y1, x2, y2)
                            curr_tuple = (cand_x1, cand_y1, x2, y2)
                            best_tuple = (best_x1_for_k, best_y1_for_k, best_x2, best_y2)
                            if curr_tuple < best_tuple:
                                best_x1_for_k = cand_x1
                                best_y1_for_k = cand_y1
                                best_x2 = x2
                                best_y2 = y2
            # Update global best if this k gives better solution
            if best_dist_for_k < best_dist_squared:
                best_dist_squared = best_dist_for_k
                best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = best_x1_for_k, best_y1_for_k, best_x1_for_k + width, best_y1_for_k + height
            elif best_dist_for_k == best_dist_squared:
                # Lexicographic comparison
                curr_tuple = (best_x1_for_k, best_y1_for_k, best_x1_for_k + width, best_y1_for_k + height)
                best_tuple = (best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)
                if curr_tuple < best_tuple:
                    best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = best_x1_for_k, best_y1_for_k, best_x1_for_k + width, best_y1_for_k + height
    
    print(best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)
```

```python
        y1_candidates.append(int(ideal_y1))
        if int(ideal_y1) - 1 >= y1_min:

        if int(ideal_y1) + 1 <= y1_max:
            y1_candidates.append(int(ideal_y1) + 1)

                best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1, y1, x2, y2

                best_tuple = (best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)
                if curr < best_tuple:
                    best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1, y1, x2, y2
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You are given a rectangle grid. That grid's size is n × m. Let's denote the coordinate system on the grid. So, each point on the grid will have coordinates — a pair of integers (x, y) (0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m).

Your task is to find a maximum sub-rectangle on the grid (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2) so that it contains the given point (x, y), and its length-width ratio is exactly (a, b). In other words the following conditions must hold: 0 ≤ x_1 ≤ x ≤ x_2 ≤ n, 0 ≤ y_1 ≤ y ≤ y_2 ≤ m, $\frac{x_{2} - x_{1}}{y_{2} - y_{1}} = \frac{a}{b}$.

The sides of this sub-rectangle should be parallel to the axes. And values x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2 should be integers. [Image] 

If there are multiple solutions, find the rectangle which is closest to (x, y). Here "closest" means the Euclid distance between (x, y) and the center of the rectangle is as small as possible. If there are still multiple solutions, find the lexicographically minimum one. Here "lexicographically minimum" means that we should consider the sub-rectangle as sequence of integers (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2), so we can choose the lexicographically minimum one.


-----Input-----

The first line contains six integers n, m, x, y, a, b (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 10^9, 0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m, 1 ≤ a ≤ n, 1 ≤ b ≤ m).


-----Output-----

Print four integers x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2, which represent the founded sub-rectangle whose left-bottom point is (x_1, y_1) and right-up point is (x_2, y_2).

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×m grid with coordinates (x,y) where 0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m
2. We need to find a sub-rectangle that:
   - Contains point (x,y)
   - Has length-width ratio exactly a/b (where length = x₂-x₁, width = y₂-y₁)
   - Is as close as possible to (x,y) in Euclidean distance to its center
   - If multiple solutions, choose lexicographically smallest

## Mathematical Analysis

The ratio constraint means: (x₂-x₁)/(y₂-y₁) = a/b

This gives us: b(x₂-x₁) = a(y₂-y₁)

Let's define:
- width = w = x₂ - x₁
- height = h = y₂ - y₁

Then: b×w = a×h, so h = (b×w)/a

For h to be an integer, w must be divisible by a (since b and a might have common factors).

Actually, since b×w = a×h, we need w to be divisible by gcd(a,b) and h to be divisible by gcd(a,b). More precisely, we need w to be divisible by a/gcd(a,b) and h by b/gcd(a,b).

Better approach: Let g = gcd(a,b), then a = g×a', b = g×b' where gcd(a',b') = 1.

Then b×w = a×h becomes (g×b')×w = (g×a')×h, so b'×w = a'×h.

Since gcd(a',b') = 1, we need w to be divisible by a' and h to be divisible by b'.

So w = k×a', h = k×b' for some positive integer k.

## Finding Valid Rectangles

For each valid k, we have:
- width = w = k×a'
- height = h = k×b'

The sub-rectangle must contain (x,y), so:
- x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₂ = x₁ + w
- y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₂ = y₁ + h

This gives:
- x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₁ + w, so x - w ≤ x₁ ≤ x
- y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₁ + h, so y - h ≤ y₁ ≤ y

Since x₁ ≥ 0 and y₁ ≥ 0, we have:
- x₁ ≥ max(0, x - w)
- x₁ ≤ min(x, x) = x
- Similarly: y₁ ≥ max(0, y - h)
- y₁ ≤ min(y, y) = y

Actually: x₁ ≤ x and x₁ ≥ x - w, so x₁ ∈ [max(0, x - w), x]
And y₁ ∈ [max(0, y - h), y]

Also, x₂ = x₁ + w ≤ n, so x₁ ≤ n - w
And y₂ = y₁ + h ≤ m, so y₁ ≤ m - h

So constraints are:
- x₁ ∈ [max(0, x - w), min(x, n - w)]
- y₁ ∈ [max(0, y - h), min(y, m - h)]

For a valid rectangle to exist with given k, we need:
- max(0, x - w) ≤ min(x, n - w)
- max(0, y - h) ≤ min(y, m - h)

## Finding Optimal k

We need to find the value of k that gives the rectangle closest to (x,y).

The center of rectangle is at ((x₁+x₂)/2, (y₁+y₂)/2) = ((x₁ + x₁ + w)/2, (y₁ + y₁ + h)/2) = (x₁ + w/2, y₁ + h/2)

Distance squared from center to (x,y): (x - (x₁ + w/2))² + (y - (y₁ + h/2))²

To minimize this distance, we want x₁ + w/2 ≈ x and y₁ + h/2 ≈ y.

So x₁ ≈ x - w/2 and y₁ ≈ y - h/2.

For our constraints, we have:
- x₁ ≥ max(0, x - w)
- x₁ ≤ min(x, n - w)

The best x₁ would be max(0, x - w, x - w/2) = max(0, x - w/2) actually since x - w/2 ≥ x - w.

Wait, let me reconsider. The distance is minimized when (x₁ + w/2) is as close as possible to x, so x₁ should be as close as possible to x - w/2.

So x₁_opt = max(0, x - w/2, x - w) = max(0, x - w/2)
And x₁_opt = min(x, n - w, x - w/2 + w) = min(x, n - w, x + w/2)

Actually: x₁ should satisfy x₁ + w/2 as close as possible to x, so x₁ as close as possible to x - w/2.

The valid range is [L_x, R_x] where:
- L_x = max(0, x - w)
- R_x = min(x, n - w)

And x₁ should be as close as possible to (x - w/2).

So if (x - w/2) ∈ [L_x, R_x], we choose x₁ = x - w/2. Otherwise, we choose the endpoint closer to x - w/2.

Similarly for y₁.

## Algorithm

1. Calculate g = gcd(a,b), a' = a//g, b' = b//g
2. For each valid k (starting from 1 upwards), calculate w = k×a', h = k×b'
3. Check if valid: w ≤ n, h ≤ m, and there exists x₁, y₁ satisfying constraints
4. Among valid k, find k that minimizes distance from center to (x,y)
5. If tie, choose lexicographically smallest (x₁,y₁,x₂,y₂)

But k can be very large (up to 10^9), so we need to be smart.

Actually, we only need to check k such that:
- w = k×a' ≤ n → k ≤ n//a'
- h = k×b' ≤ m → k ≤ m//b'

So k_max = min(n//a', m//b')

For a given k, the center is at (x₁ + w/2, y₁ + h/2). To minimize distance, we want to choose x₁ and y₁ optimally.

For x₁:
- Range: [L_x, R_x] = [max(0, x - w), min(x, n - w)]
- We want x₁ as close as possible to (x - w/2)
- So if (x - w/2) ∈ [L_x, R_x], use x₁ = x - w/2, else use the endpoint closer to x - w/2

Similarly for y₁.

Let me implement this:

```python
import math
from math import gcd

def solve():
    n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Simplify the ratio a/b
    g = gcd(a, b)
    a_prime = a // g
    b_prime = b // g
    
    # Maximum k we need to consider
    k_max = min(n // a_prime, m // b_prime)
    
    best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = None, None, None, None
    best_dist_squared = float('inf')
    
    # Try all possible k values
    for k in range(1, k_max + 1):
        w = k * a_prime  # width
        h = k * b_prime  # height
        
        # Check if rectangle can be placed
        # x1 range: from max(0, x - w) to min(x, n - w)
        x1_min = max(0, x - w)
        x1_max = min(x, n - w)
        
        # y1 range: from max(0, y - h) to min(y, m - h)
        y1_min = max(0, y - h)
        y1_max = min(y, m - h)
        
        # If ranges are valid
        if x1_min > x1_max or y1_min > y1_max:
            continue
            
        # Find optimal x1 and y1 to minimize distance to (x,y)
        # Center is at (x1 + w/2, y1 + h/2)
        # We want to minimize (x - (x1 + w/2))^2 + (y - (y1 + h/2))^2
        # This is equivalent to minimizing (x - x1 - w/2)^2 + (y - y1 - h/2)^2
        
        # Optimal x1 should be as close as possible to (x - w/2)
        x1_target = x - w / 2
        if x1_target < x1_min:
            x1_candidate = x1_min
        elif x1_target > x1_max:
            x1_candidate = x1_max
        else:
            x1_candidate = x1_target
        
        # Round to integer (since it's already in range)
        x1 = int(x1_candidate)
        
        # Optimal y1 should be as close as possible to (y - h/2)
        y1_target = y - h / 2
        if y1_target < y1_min:
            y1_candidate = y1_min
        elif y1_target > y1_max:
            y1_candidate = y1_max
        else:
            y1_candidate = y1_target
            
        y1 = int(y1_candidate)
        
        # Calculate distance squared
        center_x = x1 + w / 2
        center_y = y1 + h / 2
        dist_squared = (x - center_x) ** 2 + (y - center_y) ** 2
        
        # Check if this is better
        if dist_squared < best_dist_squared:
            best_dist_squared = dist_squared
            best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1, y1, x1 + w, y1 + h
        elif dist_squared == best_dist_squared:
            # Lexicographically smaller: compare (x1, y1, x2, y2)
            current = (x1, y1, x1 + w, y1 + h)
            best_current = (best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)
            if current < best_current:
                best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1, y1, x1 + w, y1 + h
    
    print(best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)

solve()
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You are given a rectangle grid. That grid's size is n × m. Let's denote the coordinate system on the grid. So, each point on the grid will have coordinates — a pair of integers (x, y) (0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m).

Your task is to find a maximum sub-rectangle on the grid (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2) so that it contains the given point (x, y), and its length-width ratio is exactly (a, b). In other words the following conditions must hold: 0 ≤ x_1 ≤ x ≤ x_2 ≤ n, 0 ≤ y_1 ≤ y ≤ y_2 ≤ m, $\frac{x_{2} - x_{1}}{y_{2} - y_{1}} = \frac{a}{b}$.

The sides of this sub-rectangle should be parallel to the axes. And values x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2 should be integers. [Image] 

If there are multiple solutions, find the rectangle which is closest to (x, y). Here "closest" means the Euclid distance between (x, y) and the center of the rectangle is as small as possible. If there are still multiple solutions, find the lexicographically minimum one. Here "lexicographically minimum" means that we should consider the sub-rectangle as sequence of integers (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2), so we can choose the lexicographically minimum one.


-----Input-----

The first line contains six integers n, m, x, y, a, b (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 10^9, 0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m, 1 ≤ a ≤ n, 1 ≤ b ≤ m).


-----Output-----

Print four integers x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2, which represent the founded sub-rectangle whose left-bottom point is (x_1, y_1) and right-up point is (x_2, y_2).


-----Examples-----
Input
9 9 5 5 2 1

Output
1 3 9 7

Input
100 100 52 50 46 56

Output

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×m grid with coordinates (x,y) where 0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m
2. We need to find a sub-rectangle that:
   - Contains the point (x,y)
   - Has length-width ratio exactly a:b (where length = x2-x1, width = y2-y1)
   - The rectangle sides are parallel to axes
   - x1,y1,x2,y2 are integers
3. If multiple solutions exist, we choose:
   - Closest to (x,y) by Euclidean distance to center
   - If still tied, lexicographically minimum

## Mathematical Analysis

Given the ratio condition: (x2-x1)/(y2-y1) = a/b

We can write: b(x2-x1) = a(y2-y1)

Let's say the width w = y2-y1, then length l = (a/b) * w = (a/b) * w.

Since we need integers, w must be a multiple of b for l to be integer.

So: let w = k * b, then l = k * a.

We have: x2-x1 = k*a and y2-y1 = k*b for some positive integer k.

The sub-rectangle must contain (x,y), so:
- x1 ≤ x ≤ x2
- y1 ≤ y ≤ y2

This gives us:
- x1 ≤ x ≤ x1 + k*a
- y1 ≤ y ≤ y1 + k*b

## Constraints for valid k

For the width dimension (y):
- y1 ≤ y ≤ y1 + k*b
- So: y1 ≤ y and y1 ≥ y - k*b
- Therefore: y - k*b ≤ y1 ≤ y

For the length dimension (x):
- x1 ≤ x ≤ x1 + k*a  
- So: x1 ≤ x and x1 ≥ x - k*a
- Therefore: x - k*a ≤ x1 ≤ x

We also need to ensure the rectangle stays within bounds:
- x1 ≥ 0
- x2 = x1 + k*a ≤ n
- y1 ≥ 0  
- y2 = y1 + k*b ≤ m

## Solving the problem

For each valid k, we need to:
1. Find valid ranges for x1 and y1
2. For each valid (x1,y1), calculate the center and distance
3. Choose the best one

However, k can be very large (up to 10^9), so we need to be smart.

Let's think about the constraints:
- 0 ≤ x1 ≤ x ≤ x1 + k*a ≤ n
- 0 ≤ y1 ≤ y ≤ y1 + k*b ≤ m

From x: x - k*a ≤ x1 ≤ x, and x1 ≥ 0, x1 ≤ x
So: max(0, x - k*a) ≤ x1 ≤ min(x, x)

From y: y - k*b ≤ y1 ≤ y, and y1 ≥ 0, y1 ≤ y
So: max(0, y - k*b) ≤ y1 ≤ min(y, y)

Also: x1 + k*a ≤ n → x1 ≤ n - k*a
And: y1 + k*b ≤ m → y1 ≤ m - k*b

So overall constraints:
- x1 ∈ [max(0, x - k*a), min(x, n - k*a)]
- y1 ∈ [max(0, y - k*b), min(y, m - k*b)]

For there to be a valid rectangle with ratio a:b and size k×(k*b):
- The interval for x1 must be non-empty: max(0, x - k*a) ≤ min(x, n - k*a)
- The interval for y1 must be non-empty: max(0, y - k*b) ≤ min(y, m - k*b)

## Finding valid k values

For large k, the constraints become stricter. We need to find the range of valid k.

From x: k*a ≤ n - x (since x1 ≥ x - k*a and x1 ≤ n - k*a, so x - k*a ≤ n - k*a → x ≤ n)
From y: k*b ≤ m - y (similarly)

Also, k must be such that x - k*a ≤ x and x - k*a ≥ 0 (or at least x1 ≥ 0), and y - k*b ≤ y and y - k*b ≥ 0.

Actually, let's reconsider. For k to be valid:
- k*a ≤ n - x (because x1 + k*a ≤ n and x1 ≤ x, so x + k*a ≤ n)
- k*b ≤ m - y (because y1 + k*b ≤ m and y1 ≤ y, so y + k*b ≤ m)

So: k ≤ (n - x) / a and k ≤ (m - y) / b

Also, k ≥ 0.

But k must be such that we can place the rectangle properly.

Distance from (x,y) to center: sqrt((x - (x1 + k*a/2))² + (y - (y1 + k*b/2))²)

Let g = gcd(a,b), then a = a', b = b' where a' and b' are coprime, and g = gcd(a,b).

Then k must be of the form p/(g) where p is a positive integer.

- x1 ≤ x ≤ x1 + L
- y1 ≤ y ≤ y1 + W

- y1 ≥ 0, y1 + W ≤ m

So:
- x1 ≤ x and x1 ≥ x - L
- x1 ≤ n - L
- y1 ≤ y and y1 ≥ y - W
- y1 ≤ m - W

So we need:
- max(0, x - L) ≤ x1 ≤ min(x, n - L)
- max(0, y - W) ≤ y1 ≤ min(y, m - W)

For valid p, we need both intervals to be non-empty.

Now, L = p*a/g, W = p*b/g

Constraints:
- p*a/g ≤ n - x (from x1 ≤ n - L)
- p*b/g ≤ m - y (from y1 ≤ m - W)

So p ≤ g*(n-x)/a and p ≤ g*(m-y)/b

Also p ≥ 1.

So p_max = min( floor(g*(n-x)/a), floor(g*(m-y)/b) ) and p ≥ 1.

Now for each valid p, we:
1. Calculate L = p*a/g, W = p*b/g
2. Find valid x1 range: [max(0, x - L), min(x, n - L)]
3. Find valid y1 range: [max(0, y - W), min(y, m - W)]
4. If both ranges are non-empty, calculate centers and distances
5. Choose the best solution

Since g = gcd(a,b) and a,b ≤ 10^9, g can be large, but p_max is at most around 10^9/g, which might be manageable.

Let me implement this.

```python
import math

def gcd(a, b):
    while b:
        a, b = b, a % b
    return a

# Read input
n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())

# Find gcd of a and b
g = gcd(a, b)
a_reduced = a // g
b_reduced = b // g

# Maximum possible p
max_p = min((n - x) // a_reduced, (m - y) // b_reduced)

# If no valid p, we have a problem
if max_p == 0:
    # This should not happen based on problem, but just in case
    # Try to find any valid rectangle
    # Actually, if no p, then no solution, but problem guarantees solution
    # Let's check if there's any solution
    # Perhaps we need to allow p=0? But that gives zero size
    # So we need to return something
    # Try p=1 if possible
    if (n - x) >= a_reduced and (m - y) >= b_reduced:
        p = 1
    else:
        # This is an error condition
        p = 0
else:
    p = max_p

# We need to iterate through all valid p values and find the best solution
best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = None, None, None, None
best_dist_squared = float('inf')

# The problem says if multiple solutions, choose closest to (x,y), then lexicographically smallest
# We'll iterate through valid p values
valid_p_values = []
for p in range(1, max_p + 1):
    L = p * a_reduced
    W = p * b_reduced
    
    # Calculate valid x1 range
    x1_min = max(0, x - L)
    x1_max = min(x, n - L)
    
    # Calculate valid y1 range
    y1_min = max(0, y - W)
    y1_max = min(y, m - W)
    
    # Check if valid ranges
    if x1_min <= x1_max and y1_min <= y1_max:
        valid_p_values.append((p, L, W, x1_min, x1_max, y1_min, y1_max))

# If no valid p, we have a problem
if not valid_p_values:
    # This should not happen, but for safety
    # Try the smallest p=1 if it fits
    if (n - x) >= a_reduced and (m - y) >= b_reduced:
        p = 1
        L = a_reduced
        W = b_reduced
        x1_min = max(0, x - L)
        x1_max = min(x, n - L)
        y1_min = max(0, y - W)
        y1_max = min(y, m - W)
        if x1_min <= x1_max and y1_min <= y1_max:
            valid_p_values = [(p, L, W, x1_min, x1_max, y1_min, y1_max)]
        else:
            # no solution, use default
            valid_p_values = []
    else:
        valid_p_values = []

# Find the best solution
for p, L, W, x1_min, x1_max, y1_min, y1_max in valid_p_values:
    # Try all combinations of x1, y1 in the valid ranges
    # For lexicographic order, we try x1 from min to max, y1 from min to max
    # But to get the closest to (x,y), we need to minimize distance
    
    # The center is at (x1 + L/2, y1 + W/2)
    # We want to minimize distance from (x,y) to center
    
    # For fixed p, we want to choose x1 and y1 such that the center is closest to (x,y)
    # For x1: optimal is x - L/2, but constrained to [x1_min, x1_max]
    # For y1: optimal is y - W/2, but constrained to [y1_min, y1_max]
    
    optimal_x1 = x - L / 2
    optimal_y1 = y - W / 2
    
    # Check bounds
    candidate_x1 = max(x1_min, min(x1_max, optimal_x1))
    candidate_y1 = max(y1_min, min(y1_max, optimal_y1))
    
    # This might not be optimal if the center is outside the space, so we check all corners? 
    # Actually for convex, the closest point in the rectangle is the projection, so this should work.
    
    # Validate candidate is in range
    if x1_min <= candidate_x1 <= x1_max and y1_min <= candidate_y1 <= y1_max:
        center_x = candidate_x1 + L / 2
        center_y = candidate_y1 + W / 2
        dist_squared = (x - center_x) ** 2 + (y - center_y) ** 2
        
        # Check if this is better
        if dist_squared < best_dist_squared:
            best_dist_squared = dist_squared
            best_x1 = int(candidate_x1)
            best_y1 = int(candidate_y1)
            best_x2 = best_x1 + L
            best_y2 = best_y1 + W
        elif dist_squared == best_dist_squared:
            # Lexicographically minimum - we need to compare (x1, y1, x2, y2)
            # But we're iterating p, so we need to handle this
            # Actually, we're going in order of p, so we want smallest x1, then y1
            # But we need to make sure we get the lexicographically minimum
            
            # Check if current candidate is lexicographically smaller
            current = (best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)
            candidate = (int(candidate_x1), int(candidate_y1), best_x1 + L, best_y1 + W)
            if candidate < current:
                best_x1 = candidate[0]
                best_y1 = candidate[1]
                best_x2 = candidate[2]
                best_y2 = candidate[3]
    else:
        # The projected point is outside, so we take corner that minimizes distance
        # Check corners
        corners = [
            (x1_min, y1_min),
            (x1_min, y1_max),
            (x1_max, y1_min),
            (x1_max, y1_max)
        ]
        
        for x1, y1 in corners:
            if x1_min <= x1 <= x1_max and y1_min <= y1 <= y1_max:
                center_x = x1 + L / 2
                center_y = y1 + W / 2
                dist_squared = (x - center_x) ** 2 + (y - center_y) ** 2
                
                if dist_squared < best_dist_squared:
                    best_dist_squared = dist_squared
                    best_x1 = x1
                    best_y1 = y1
                    best_x2 = x1 + L
                    best_y2 = y1 + W
                elif dist_squared == best_dist_squared:
                    # Lexicographic comparison
                    current = (best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)
                    candidate = (x1, y1, x1 + L, y1 + W)
                    if candidate < current:
                        best_x1 = x1
                        best_y1 = y1
                        best_x2 = x1 + L
                        best_y2 = y1 + W

# If we found a solution, output it
if best_x1 is not None:
    print(best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)
else:
    # Fallback - should not happen
    # Try to return a rectangle that contains (x,y) with ratio a:b
    # Use p=1 if possible
    if (n - x) >= a_reduced and (m - y) >= b_reduced:
        p = 1
        L = a_reduced
        W = b_reduced
        x1_min = max(0, x - L)
        x1_max = min(x, n - L)
        y1_min = max(0, y - W)
        y1_max = min(y, m - W)
        if x1_min <= x1_max and y1_min <= y1_max:
            x1 = x1_min
            y1 = y1_min
            print(x1, y1, x1 + L, y1 + W)
    else:
        print(0, 0, n, m)
```

Wait, this is getting complex. Let me simplify and check the first example.

Example 1: n=9, m=9, x=5, y=5, a=2, b=1
- g = gcd(2,1) = 1
- a_reduced = 2, b_reduced = 1
- max_p = min((9-5)//2, (9-5)//1) = min(4//2, 4//1) = min(2,4) = 2

p values: 1,2

p=1: L=2, W=1
- x1_min = max(0,5-2)=3, x1_max=min(5,9-2)=5
- y1_min = max(0,5-1)=4, y1_max=min(5,9-1)=5
- Optimal x1: 5 - 1 = 4, optimal y1: 5 - 0.5 = 4.5
- So x1=4, y1=5? But y1_max=5, y1_min=4, so y1=5
- Center: (4+1, 5+0.5) = (5,5.5), distance to (5,5): 0.5
- Rectangle: (4,4) to (6,5)

p=2: L=4, W=2
- x1_min = max(0,5-4)=1, x1_max=min(5,9-4)=5
- y1_min = max(0,5-2)=3, y1_max=min(5,9-2)=5
- Optimal x1: 5-2=3, optimal y1: 5-1=4
- So x1=3, y1=4
- Center: (3+2, 4+1) = (5,5), distance to (5,5): 0
- Distance 0 is better!

So answer should be x1=3, y1=4, x2=7, y2=6

But expected output is "1 3 9 7"

Check: (1,3) to (9,7): length = 8, width = 4, ratio = 8/4 = 2/1 = a/b ✓
Contains (5,5)? Yes
Center: (5,5), distance to (5,5): 0 ✓

So both have distance 0 to center! 

Now we need to choose lexicographically smallest.

For p=2: (3,4,7,6)
For the other option with p=2, we also have choices.

For p=2, we found optimum at (3,4) with center (5,5), distance 0.

For the rectangle (1,3) to (9,7): x1=1, y1=3, x2=9, y2=7
Center: (5,5), distance 0

Both have same distance, so we choose lexicographically smallest.

(1,3) vs (3,4): (1,3) is lexicographically smaller.

So the answer should be (1,3,9,7).

How did we get (1,3)? 

For p=2: x1_min = max(0,5-4)=1, x1_max=5, so we could choose x1=1
y1_min=3, y1_max=5, so we could choose y1=3

In our calculation, we chose the optimal point (3,4) because it's closest to (5,5), but we have multiple solutions with distance 0.

We need to find all solutions with minimal distance, and among those, pick lexicographically smallest.

In this case, both have center (5,5) and distance 0, so we want lexicographically smallest (x1,y1).

For p=2:
- x1 in [1,5], y1 in [3,5]
- center at (x1+2, y1+1)
- when is center at (5,5)? When x1+2=5 and y1+1=5 → x1=3, y1=4

So only one point has center at (5,5).

Wait, that's not right. For center to be at (5,5): 
- x1 + 2 = 5 → x1 = 3
- y1 + 1 = 5 → y1 = 4

Only (3,4) gives center (5,5).

But (1,3) to (9,7) gives center (5,5) - how?

(1,3) to (9,7): x2-x1 = 8, y2-y1 = 4
Length = 8, width = 4, ratio 2:1 ✓
Center: ( (1+9)/2, (3+7)/2 ) = (5,5) ✓

But with p=2, we have length = 4, width = 2, not 8,4.

So 8,4 is not of the form p*2, p*1 for p=2 (that would be 4,2).

So 8,4 corresponds to p=4.

Let me check p_max: (9-5)//2 = 4//2 = 2, so p_max = 2.

But 8/2 = 4, so p=4, but 4 > 2.

I see the error. In the constraint derivation:

p*a/g ≤ n-x → p*2 ≤ 9-5 = 4 → p ≤ 2

But 8/2 = 4, so p=4 would give length 8, which is not ≤ 4.

The constraint should be p*a/g ≤ n-x → p*2 ≤ 4 → p ≤ 2.

So p=4 is not valid.

But the example output has length 8, which is not valid.

Let me double-check the example.

The example says: "1 3 9 7"

Length = 9-1 = 8, width = 7-3 = 4, ratio 8/4 = 2/1 = a/b ✓
Contains (5,5)? Yes
Center: (5,5) ✓

But with our derivation, p should be such that length = p*a/g = p*2/g, g=gcd(2,1)=1, so length = 2p.

So 2p = 8 → p = 4.

But constraint: p ≤ (n-x)/a_reduced = (9-5)/2 = 4/2 = 2.

So p ≤ 2.

This suggests our constraint is wrong.

Let me re-derive the constraint.

We have x1 in [max(0, x-L), min(x, n-L)] where L = p*a/g.

For the rectangle to fit: x1 + L ≤ n → x1 ≤ n - L

So we need max(0, x-L) ≤ n - L

This is true if x-L ≤ n - L (which is x ≤ n) and x-L ≥ 0 (if L ≤ x) or if x-L < 0 then max(0,x-L)=0, and 0 ≤ n-L → L ≤ n.

The condition is always true if L ≤ n.

But we also need x1 ≥ x-L, and x1 ≤ n-L, so we need x-L ≤ n-L → x ≤ n, which is true.

And we need x-L ≤ n-L, which is true.

The real constraint is from the range: x1 in [max(0,x-L), min(x, n-L)]

This is non-empty if max(0,x-L) ≤ min(x, n-L)

Cases:
- If x-L ≤ 0, then max(0,x-L)=0, and min(x,n-L) ≥ 0, so we need 0 ≤ min(x,n-L) → which is true since x≥0, n-L≥0 if L≤n
- If x-L > 0, then max= x-L, min= min(x,n-L)

So we need x-L ≤ min(x, n-L)

This is true if x-L ≤ x (always true) and x-L ≤ n-L → x ≤ n (true)

So the constraint is always satisfied if L ≤ n and L ≤ m (similarly for y).

For the example, with length 8: L = 8, a_reduced = 2, g=1, so p = 8/2 = 4.

But n-x = 4, and a_reduced = 2, so (n-x)/a_reduced = 4/2 = 2.

We need p ≤ 2.

So p=4 > 2, not allowed.

But the example output should be valid.

I think I see the mistake. The condition should be that the rectangle must contain (x,y), so we need x1 ≤ x ≤ x1 + L, which means x - L ≤ x1 ≤ x.

And x1 + L ≤ n, so x1 ≤ n - L.

So we need x - L ≤ n - L → x ≤ n, true.

But we also need x - L ≤ x, true.

The lower bound for x1 is max(0, x - L)

The upper bound is min(x, n - L)

This is non-empty if max(0, x - L) ≤ min(x, n - L)

This is true if x - L ≤ n - L (x ≤ n) and x - L ≤ x (true), and 0 ≤ min(x, n - L) (true if L ≤ n)

So the only constraint is L ≤ n and L ≤ m.

For the example: L=8 ≤ 9, W=4 ≤ 9, so it should be allowed.

But earlier I derived p ≤ (n-x)/a_reduced, which is not correct.

Let me go back.

From the valid x1 range: max(0, x-L) ≤ min(x, n-L)

This means: x-L ≤ n-L (x ≤ n) and x-L ≤ x (true), and if x-L > 0 then x-L ≤ min(x, n-L)

If x-L > 0, then x-L ≤ min(x, n-L) → x-L ≤ n-L → x ≤ n, true.

So the only constraints are L ≤ n and L ≤ m.

For y: W ≤ m.

So we only need that the length and width are within bounds.

For the example: L=8 ≤ 9, W=4 ≤ 9, so valid.

For the ratio: (x2-x1)/(y2-y1) = 8/4 = 2/1 = a/b.

So the only constraint is that the dimensions are within bounds.

The value of p is not bounded by (n-x)/a_reduced, only by L ≤ n, W ≤ m.

Since L = p*a/g, we need p*a/g ≤ n → p ≤ g*n/a
Similarly, p*b/g ≤ m → p ≤ g*m/b

So p_max = min( floor(g*n/a), floor(g*m/b) )

For example 1: g=1, a=2, b=1, n=9, m=9
p_max = min( floor(1*9/2), floor(1*9/1) ) = min(4,9) = 4

p=1,2,3,4

For p=4: L=8, W=4
x1 in [max(0,5-8), min(5,9-8)] = [max(0,-3), min(5,1)] = [0,1]
y1 in [max(0,5-4), min(5,9-4)] = [max(0,1), min(5,5)] = [1,5]

For center at (5,5): 
- x1 + 4 = 5 → x1 = 1
- y1 + 2 = 5 → y1 = 3

So (x1=1, y1=3) is valid.

Distance to (5,5): 0.

Other solutions with distance 0 would be when center is (5,5), which only happens at x1=1, y1=3.

Other solutions with center at (5,5) only from this.

So answer is (1,3,9,7) ✓

I had the constraint wrong. It's not about (n-x)/a, it's about the total size.

The correct approach:

```python
import math

def gcd(a, b):
    while b:
        a, b = b, a % b
    return a

# Read input
n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())

# Find gcd of a and b
g = gcd(a, b)
a_reduced = a // g
b_reduced = b // g

# Maximum possible p such that the dimensions fit within bounds
# Length = p * a_reduced, Width = p * b_reduced
# We need: p * a_reduced <= n and p * b_reduced <= m
p_max = min(n // a_reduced, m // b_reduced) if a_reduced > 0 else n // 1
if a_reduced == 0:
    a_reduced = 1
if b_reduced == 0:
    b_reduced = 1
p_max = min(n // a_reduced, m // b_reduced)

# If no valid p, return something (should not happen)
if p_max == 0:
    # Try to find any solution
    p = 1
    if (n >= a_reduced) and (m >= b_reduced):
        p = 1
    else:
        p = 0

# We'll iterate through all possible p values
best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = None, None, None, None
best_dist_squared = float('inf')

# For each p from 1 to p_max
for p in range(1, p_max + 1):
    L = p * a_reduced
    W = p * b_reduced
    
    # Calculate valid x1 range
    x1_min = max(0, x - L)
    x1_max = min(x, n - L)
    
    # Calculate valid y1 range
    y1_min = max(0, y - W)
    y1_max = min(y, m - W)
    
    # Check if valid ranges
    if x1_min <= x1_max and y1_min <= y1_max:
        # The center of the rectangle is at (x1 + L/2, y1 + W/2)
        # We want to minimize distance to (x,y)
        
        # For fixed (L,W), find (x1,y1) that minimizes distance
        # This is minimized when x1 is as close as possible to x - L/2
        # and y1 is as close as possible to y - W/2
        
        opt_x1 = x - L / 2
        opt_y1 = y - W / 2
        
        # Clamp to valid ranges
        candidate_x1 = max(x1_min, min(x1_max, opt_x1))
        candidate_y1 = max(y1_min, min(y1_max, opt_y1))
        
        # Validate that candidate is in range
        if x1_min <= candidate_x1 <= x1_max and y1_min <= candidate_y1 <= y1_max:
            center_x = candidate_x1 + L / 2
            center_y = candidate_y1 + W / 2
            dist_squared = (x - center_x) ** 2 + (y - center_y) ** 2
            
            if dist_squared < best_dist_squared:
                best_dist_squared = dist_squared
                best_x1 = int(candidate_x1)
                best_y1 = int(candidate_y1)
                best_x2 = best_x1 + L
                best_y2 = best_y1 + W
            elif dist_squared == best_dist_squared:
                # Lexicographically smaller
                current = (best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)
                candidate = (int(candidate_x1), int(candidate_y1), best_x1 + L, best_y1 + W)
                if candidate < current:
                    best_x1 = candidate[0]
                    best_y1 = candidate[1]
                    best_x2 = candidate[2]
                    best_y2 = candidate[3]
        else:
            # Try all corners of the valid rectangle
            corners = [
                (x1_min, y1_min),
                (x1_min, y1_max),
                (x1_max, y1_min),
                (x1_max, y1_max)
            ]
            for x1, y1 in corners:
                if x1_min <= x1 <= x1_max and y1_min <= y1 <= y1_max:
                    center_x = x1 + L / 2
                    center_y = y1 + W / 2
                    dist_squared = (x - center_x) ** 2 + (y - center_y) ** 2
                    
                    if dist_squared < best_dist_squared:
                        best_dist_squared = dist_squared
                        best_x1 = x1
                        best_y1 = y1
                        best_x2 = x1 + L
                        best_y2 = y1 + W
                    elif dist_squared == best_dist_squared:
                        current = (best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)
                        candidate = (x1, y1, x1 + L, y1 + W)
                        if candidate < current:
                            best_x1 = x1
                            best_y1 = y1
                            best_x2 = x1 + L
                            best_y2 = y1 + W

# Output the result
print(best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)
```

Submitted Code

View Extracted Code

def gcd(a, b):
    while b:
        a, b = b, a % b
    return a

# Read input

# Length = p * a_reduced, Width = p * b_reduced

if a_reduced == 0:
    a_reduced = 1
if b_reduced == 0:
    b_reduced = 1

    # Try to find any solution
    p = 1
    if (n >= a_reduced) and (m >= b_reduced):
        p = 1
    else:
        p = 0

    x1_min = max(0, x - L)

    # Calculate valid y1 range
    y1_min = max(0, y - W)
    y1_max = min(y, m - W)

    if x1_min <= x1_max and y1_min <= y1_max:
        # The center of the rectangle is at (x1 + L/2, y1 + W/2)
        # We want to minimize distance to (x,y)
        
        # For fixed (L,W), find (x1,y1) that minimizes distance
        # This is minimized when x1 is as close as possible to x - L/2
        # and y1 is as close as possible to y - W/2
        
        opt_x1 = x - L / 2
        opt_y1 = y - W / 2
        
        # Clamp to valid ranges
        candidate_x1 = max(x1_min, min(x1_max, opt_x1))
        candidate_y1 = max(y1_min, min(y1_max, opt_y1))
        
        # Validate that candidate is in range
        if x1_min <= candidate_x1 <= x1_max and y1_min <= candidate_y1 <= y1_max:
            center_x = candidate_x1 + L / 2
            center_y = candidate_y1 + W / 2
            dist_squared = (x - center_x) ** 2 + (y - center_y) ** 2

                best_dist_squared = dist_squared
                best_x1 = int(candidate_x1)
                best_y1 = int(candidate_y1)
                best_x2 = best_x1 + L
                best_y2 = best_y1 + W
            elif dist_squared == best_dist_squared:
                # Lexicographically smaller
                current = (best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)
                candidate = (int(candidate_x1), int(candidate_y1), best_x1 + L, best_y1 + W)
                if candidate < current:
                    best_x1 = candidate[0]
                    best_y1 = candidate[1]
                    best_x2 = candidate[2]
                    best_y2 = candidate[3]
        else:
            # Try all corners of the valid rectangle
            corners = [
                (x1_min, y1_min),
                (x1_min, y1_max),
                (x1_max, y1_min),
                (x1_max, y1_max)
            ]
            for x1, y1 in corners:
                if x1_min <= x1 <= x1_max and y1_min <= y1 <= y1_max:
                    center_x = x1 + L / 2
                    center_y = y1 + W / 2
                    dist_squared = (x - center_x) ** 2 + (y - center_y) ** 2
                    
                    if dist_squared < best_dist_squared:
                        best_dist_squared = dist_squared
                        best_x1 = x1
                        best_y1 = y1
                        best_x2 = x1 + L
                        best_y2 = y1 + W
                    elif dist_squared == best_dist_squared:
                        current = (best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)
                        candidate = (x1, y1, x1 + L, y1 + W)
                        if candidate < current:
                            best_x1 = x1
                            best_y1 = y1
                            best_x2 = x1 + L
                            best_y2 = y1 + W

# Output the result
print(best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You are given a rectangle grid. That grid's size is n × m. Let's denote the coordinate system on the grid. So, each point on the grid will have coordinates — a pair of integers (x, y) (0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m).

Your task is to find a maximum sub-rectangle on the grid (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2) so that it contains the given point (x, y), and its length-width ratio is exactly (a, b). In other words the following conditions must hold: 0 ≤ x_1 ≤ x ≤ x_2 ≤ n, 0 ≤ y_1 ≤ y ≤ y_2 ≤ m, $\frac{x_{2} - x_{1}}{y_{2} - y_{1}} = \frac{a}{b}$.

The sides of this sub-rectangle should be parallel to the axes. And values x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2 should be integers. [Image] 

If there are multiple solutions, find the rectangle which is closest to (x, y). Here "closest" means the Euclid distance between (x, y) and the center of the rectangle is as small as possible. If there are still multiple solutions, find the lexicographically minimum one. Here "lexicographically minimum" means that we should consider the sub-rectangle as sequence of integers (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2), so we can choose the lexicographically minimum one.


-----Input-----

The first line contains six integers n, m, x, y, a, b (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 10^9, 0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m, 1 ≤ a ≤ n, 1 ≤ b ≤ m).


-----Output-----

Print four integers x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2, which represent the founded sub-rectangle whose left-bottom point is (x_1, y_1) and right-up point is (x_2, y_2).

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×m grid with coordinates (x,y) where 0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m
2. We need to find a sub-rectangle that:
   - Contains the point (x,y)
   - Has length-width ratio exactly a:b (where length = x₂-x₁, width = y₂-y₁)
   - Is as close as possible to (x,y) in Euclidean distance
   - If tie, choose lexicographically smallest (x₁,y₁,x₂,y₂)

## Key Insights

1. For ratio a:b, we have (x₂-x₁)/(y₂-y₁) = a/b, which means b(x₂-x₁) = a(y₂-y₁)
2. Let the width be w = x₂-x₁ and height be h = y₂-y₁, so b×w = a×h
3. This means w/h = a/b, so w = k×a and h = k×b for some positive integer k
4. We need 0 ≤ x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₂ ≤ n and 0 ≤ y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₂ ≤ m

## Step-by-step Solution

1. **Find valid k values**: For each possible k, we get width = k×a, height = k×b
2. **Constraints**: 
   - x₁ ≥ 0, x₂ = x₁ + k×a ≤ n, so x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₁ + k×a, so x₁ ≥ max(0, x - k×a)
   - y₁ ≥ 0, y₂ = y₁ + k×b ≤ m, so y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₁ + k×b, so y₁ ≥ max(0, y - k×b)
3. **Valid x₁ range**: max(0, x - k×a) ≤ x₁ ≤ min(x, n - k×a)
4. **Valid y₁ range**: max(0, y - k×b) ≤ y₁ ≤ min(y, m - k×b)

For each valid k, we need to find x₁ and y₁ such that:
- x₁ is in [max(0, x - k×a), min(x, n - k×a)]
- y₁ is in [max(0, y - k×b), min(y, m - k×b)]
- The distance from (x,y) to center ((x₁+x₂)/2, (y₁+y₂)/2) is minimized
- If tie, choose lexicographically smallest

## Optimization

Since n and m can be large, we don't need to check all possible k values. The maximum possible k is limited by:
- k ≤ n/a (from x₂ ≤ n)
- k ≤ m/b (from y₂ ≤ m)

So k_max = min(n//a, m//b)

For each valid k, we can find the optimal x₁ and y₁:

- To minimize distance to center, we want center as close as possible to (x,y)
- Center is at ((x₁ + x₁ + k×a)/2, (y₁ + y₁ + k×b)/2) = (x₁ + k×a/2, y₁ + k×b/2)
- Distance squared: (x₁ + k×a/2 - x)² + (y₁ + k×b/2 - y)²

To minimize this, we want x₁ close to x - k×a/2 and y₁ close to y - k×b/2, but constrained by the ranges.

## Implementation

```python
def solve():
    n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Maximum possible k value
    k_max = min(n // a, m // b)
    
    best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = None, None, None, None
    min_distance_squared = float('inf')
    
    # Try all possible k values
    for k in range(1, k_max + 1):
        width = k * a
        height = k * b
        
        # Valid range for x1: must contain x, and fit in [0, n]
        x1_min = max(0, x - width)  # x1 >= x - width to ensure x <= x1 + width
        x1_max = min(x, n - width)  # x1 <= x and x1 + width <= n
        
        # Valid range for y1: must contain y, and fit in [0, m]
        y1_min = max(0, y - height)  # y1 >= y - height to ensure y <= y1 + height
        y1_max = min(y, m - height)  # y1 <= y and y1 + height <= m
        
        # If no valid ranges, skip this k
        if x1_min > x1_max or y1_min > y1_max:
            continue
            
        # Find optimal x1 and y1 for this k
        # Center should be as close as possible to (x, y)
        # Center is at (x1 + width/2, y1 + height/2)
        
        # Optimal x1 should be close to x - width/2
        opt_x1 = x - width / 2
        x1_candidate = int(max(x1_min, min(x1_max, opt_x1)))
        
        # We also need to consider the boundaries
        x1_candidates = [x1_min, x1_max]
        if x1_min < x1_max:
            x1_candidates.append(int(x1_min + 0.5))  # round to nearest
            x1_candidates.append(int(x1_min + 0.6))  # try to move toward center
            x1_candidates.append(int(x1_min + 0.4))  # try to move toward center
            if x1_min + 1 <= x1_max:
                x1_candidates.append(x1_min + 1)
        x1_candidates = [x for x in x1_candidates if x1_min <= x <= x1_max]
        
        if not x1_candidates:
            continue
            
        # Similar for y1
        opt_y1 = y - height / 2
        y1_candidate = int(max(y1_min, min(y1_max, opt_y1)))
        
        y1_candidates = [y1_min, y1_max]
        if y1_min < y1_max:
            y1_candidates.append(int(y1_min + 0.5))
            y1_candidates.append(int(y1_min + 0.6))
            y1_candidates.append(int(y1_min + 0.4))
            if y1_min + 1 <= y1_max:
                y1_candidates.append(y1_min + 1)
        y1_candidates = [y for y in y1_candidates if y1_min <= y <= y1_max]
        
        if not y1_candidates:
            continue
            
        # Try all combinations of x1 and y1
        for x1 in x1_candidates:
            for y1 in y1_candidates:
                x2 = x1 + width
                y2 = y1 + height
                
                # Calculate center distance
                cx = (x1 + x2) / 2
                cy = (y1 + y2) / 2
                distance_squared = (cx - x) ** 2 + (cy - y) ** 2
                
                # Check if this is better
                if (best_x1 is None or 
                    distance_squared < min_distance_squared or 
                    (distance_squared == min_distance_squared and 
                     (x1 < best_x1 or (x1 == best_x1 and y1 < best_y1)))):
                    
                    best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1, y1, x2, y2
                    min_distance_squared = distance_squared
    
    # If no solution found, try the simplest one (shouldn't happen with valid input)
    if best_x1 is None:
        # Fallback: use minimum possible
        best_x1, best_y1 = 0, 0
        best_x2, best_y2 = a, b
    
    print(best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)

solve()
```

```python
# Maximum k value

    width = k * a
    height = k * b
    
    # Valid range for x1: must contain x
    x1_min = max(0, x - width)
    x1_max = min(x, n - width)
    
    # Valid range for y1: must contain y  
    y1_min = max(0, y - height)
    y1_max = min(y, m - height)
    
    # If no valid ranges, skip
    if x1_min > x1_max or y1_min > y1_max:
        continue
        
    # Find optimal x1 and y1
    # Center should be as close as possible to (x,y)
    # Center is at (x1 + width/2, y1 + height/2)

    opt_x1 = x - width / 2
    x1_choice = int(max(x1_min, min(x1_max, opt_x1)))
    
    # We need to check boundaries and nearby values
    x1_candidates = []
    for dx in [-1, 0, 1]:
        x1_candidate = x1_min + dx
        if x1_min <= x1_candidate <= x1_max:
            x1_candidates.append(x1_candidate)
    
    # Similarly for y1
    opt_y1 = y - height / 2
    y1_choice = int(max(y1_min, min(y1_max, opt_y1)))

    for dy in [-1, 0, 1]:
        y1_candidate = y1_min + dy
        if y1_min <= y1_candidate <= y1_max:
            y1_candidates.append(y1_candidate)
    
    # Try all combinations
    for x1 in x1_candidates:
        for y1 in y1_candidates:
            x2 = x1 + width
            y2 = y1 + height
            
            # Calculate distance to center
            cx = (x1 + x2) / 2
            cy = (y1 + y2) / 2
            dist_sq = (cx - x) ** 2 + (cy - y) ** 2
            
            # Update best solution

                best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1, y1, x2, y2
                min_distance_squared = dist_sq
```

### Answer: (use the provided format with backticks)

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×m grid with coordinates (x,y) where 0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m
2. We need to find a sub-rectangle that:
   - Contains the point (x,y)
   - Has length-width ratio exactly a/b (meaning (x₂-x₁)/(y₂-y₁) = a/b)
   - Is closest to (x,y) in terms of Euclidean distance to its center
   - If multiple solutions, choose lexicographically smallest

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Mathematical Analysis

Given the ratio condition: (x₂-x₁)/(y₂-y₁) = a/b

This means: b(x₂-x₁) = a(y₂-y₁)

We need to find integers x₁, y₁, x₂, y₂ such that:
- 0 ≤ x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₂ ≤ n
- 0 ≤ y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₂ ≤ m
- b(x₂-x₁) = a(y₂-y₁)

### Step 2: Parameterization

Let w = x₂ - x₁ (width) and h = y₂ - y₁ (height)
We need w/h = a/b, so w = a·k and h = b·k for some positive integer k.

So: x₂ - x₁ = a·k and y₂ - y₁ = b·k

### Step 3: Constraints on k

For the sub-rectangle to contain (x,y):
- x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₂ → x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₁ + a·k → x - a·k ≤ x₁ ≤ x
- y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₂ → y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₁ + b·k → y - b·k ≤ y₁ ≤ y

Also: 
- x₁ ≥ 0 → x - a·k ≤ x₁ ≤ x and x₁ ≥ 0
- x₂ ≤ n → x₁ + a·k ≤ n → x₁ ≤ n - a·k
- y₁ ≥ 0 → y - b·k ≤ y₁ ≤ y and y₁ ≥ 0
- y₂ ≤ m → y₁ + b·k ≤ m → y₁ ≤ m - b·k

### Step 4: Finding Valid k Values

We need to find k such that there exists x₁, y₁ satisfying all constraints.

From x₁ constraints: max(0, x - a·k) ≤ x₁ ≤ min(x, n - a·k)
From y₁ constraints: max(0, y - b·k) ≤ y₁ ≤ min(y, m - b·k)

For solution to exist: max(0, x - a·k) ≤ min(x, n - a·k) and max(0, y - b·k) ≤ min(y, m - b·k)

Simplifying: 
- x - a·k ≤ n - a·k → x ≤ n (always true)
- x ≥ 0 (always true if x ≥ 0)
- x - a·k ≤ x (always true)
- x - a·k ≤ n - a·k → x ≤ n (true)

Actually, we need: x - a·k ≤ n - a·k → x ≤ n (true)
And: x - a·k ≥ 0 → k ≤ x/a
And: x ≤ x + a·k → always true

Similarly for y: k ≤ y/b

So maximum k is min(x//a, y//b, (n-x)//a, (m-y)//b)

### Step 5: Finding Optimal k

We need to maximize the "closeness" to (x,y). The center of the rectangle is at ((x₁+x₂)/2, (y₁+y₂)/2) = ((x₁+x₁+a·k)/2, (y₁+y₁+b·k)/2) = (x₁ + a·k/2, y₁ + b·k/2)

Distance to (x,y): √[(x - (x₁ + a·k/2))² + (y - (y₁ + b·k/2))²]

To minimize distance, we want to minimize |x - (x₁ + a·k/2)| and |y - (y₁ + b·k/2)|.

For fixed k, optimal x₁ is the value that minimizes |x - (x₁ + a·k/2)| within the bounds.
Similarly for y₁.

### Step 6: Algorithm

1. Find all possible k values (from 1 to max possible)
2. For each k, find valid x₁, y₁ ranges
3. For each valid k, compute the center and distance
4. Choose k that minimizes distance, then lexicographically smallest

But since n,m up to 10^9, we can't iterate over all k.

Actually, we can find the optimal k by considering that the distance function is convex.

The center should be as close as possible to (x,y).

For a fixed k, the optimal x₁ is:
- If x ≥ a·k/2, then x₁ = max(0, x - a·k/2) to minimize distance
- But we also have constraints: x₁ ≥ max(0, x - a·k) and x₁ ≤ min(x, n - a·k)

Wait, let me reconsider.

For distance minimization, the optimal x₁ should be as close as possible to x - a·k/2.

Given the constraints, x₁ ∈ [max(0, x - a·k), min(x, n - a·k)]

So optimal x₁ = max(0, x - a·k), but capped by min(x, n - a·k).

Similarly for y₁.

### Step 7: Implementation

```python
import math

def solve():
    n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Maximum possible k values
    # k must satisfy: x >= a*k (for x1 >= 0) and y >= b*k (for y1 >= 0)
    # and k <= (n-x)/a and k <= (m-y)/b
    
    max_k = 0
    if a > 0:
        max_k = min(x // a, (n - x) // a, y // b, (m - y) // b)
    
    if max_k == 0:
        # No valid rectangle possible
        print(0, 0, 0, 0)
        return
    
    best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = None, None, None, None
    best_dist_sq = float('inf')
    
    # Try all possible k values from 1 to max_k
    # But we need to be efficient - note that the distance function is convex
    # So we can check only critical points
    
    # Actually, since max_k can be large, we need to be smarter
    
    # For each k, compute optimal x1, y1
    # x1 should be as close as possible to x - a*k/2
    # y1 should be as close as possible to y - b*k/2
    
    for k in range(1, max_k + 1):
        # Width = a*k, height = b*k
        
        # x1 constraints: max(0, x - a*k) <= x1 <= min(x, n - a*k)
        x1_min = max(0, x - a * k)
        x1_max = min(x, n - a * k)
        
        # y1 constraints: max(0, y - b*k) <= y1 <= min(y, m - b*k)
        y1_min = max(0, y - b * k)
        y1_max = min(y, m - b * k)
        
        # If no valid range, skip
        if x1_min > x1_max or y1_min > y1_max:
            continue
            
        # Find optimal x1 to minimize distance to center
        # Center x = x1 + a*k/2
        # We want to minimize |x - (x1 + a*k/2)|
        # This happens when x1 is as close as possible to x - a*k/2
        
        x1_opt = x1_min
        if x1_min <= x1_max:
            # Optimal value is the one closest to x - a*k/2
            target_x1 = x - a * k / 2.0
            if target_x1 >= x1_min and target_x1 <= x1_max:
                x1_opt = int(round(target_x1))
            else:
                if target_x1 < x1_min:
                    x1_opt = x1_min
                else:
                    x1_opt = x1_max
        
        # Check if x1_opt is within bounds
        if x1_opt < x1_min or x1_opt > x1_max:
            x1_opt = x1_min if x1_min <= x1_max else x1_max
            
        if x1_opt < x1_min or x1_opt > x1_max:
            continue
            
        # Similarly for y1
        target_y1 = y - b * k / 2.0
        y1_opt = y1_min
        if target_y1 >= y1_min and target_y1 <= y1_max:
            y1_opt = int(round(target_y1))
        else:
            if target_y1 < y1_min:
                y1_opt = y1_min
            else:
                y1_opt = y1_max
                
        if y1_opt < y1_min or y1_opt > y1_max:
            continue
            
        # Calculate distance to center
        cx = x1_opt + a * k / 2.0
        cy = y1_opt + b * k / 2.0
        dist_sq = (x - cx) ** 2 + (y - cy) ** 2
        
        if dist_sq < best_dist_sq:
            best_dist_sq = dist_sq
            best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1_opt, y1_opt, x1_opt + a * k, y1_opt + b * k
        elif dist_sq == best_dist_sq:
            # Lexicographically minimum: compare (x1,y1,x2,y2)
            current = (x1_opt, y1_opt, x1_opt + a * k, y1_opt + b * k)
            best_current = (best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)
            if current < best_current:
                best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1_opt, y1_opt, x1_opt + a * k, y1_opt + b * k
    
    print(int(best_x1), int(best_y1), int(best_x2), int(best_y2))
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You are given a rectangle grid. That grid's size is n × m. Let's denote the coordinate system on the grid. So, each point on the grid will have coordinates — a pair of integers (x, y) (0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m).

Your task is to find a maximum sub-rectangle on the grid (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2) so that it contains the given point (x, y), and its length-width ratio is exactly (a, b). In other words the following conditions must hold: 0 ≤ x_1 ≤ x ≤ x_2 ≤ n, 0 ≤ y_1 ≤ y ≤ y_2 ≤ m, $\frac{x_{2} - x_{1}}{y_{2} - y_{1}} = \frac{a}{b}$.

The sides of this sub-rectangle should be parallel to the axes. And values x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2 should be integers. [Image] 

If there are multiple solutions, find the rectangle which is closest to (x, y). Here "closest" means the Euclid distance between (x, y) and the center of the rectangle is as small as possible. If there are still multiple solutions, find the lexicographically minimum one. Here "lexicographically minimum" means that we should consider the sub-rectangle as sequence of integers (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2), so we can choose the lexicographically minimum one.


-----Input-----

The first line contains six integers n, m, x, y, a, b (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 10^9, 0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m, 1 ≤ a ≤ n, 1 ≤ b ≤ m).


-----Output-----

Print four integers x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2, which represent the founded sub-rectangle whose left-bottom point is (x_1, y_1) and right-up point is (x_2, y_2).


-----Examples-----
Input
9 9 5 5 2 1

Output
1 3 9 7

Input
100 100 52 50 46 56

Output
17 8 86 92### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
