### Answer: (use the provided format with backticks)

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×m grid with coordinates (x,y) where 0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m
2. We need to find a sub-rectangle that:
   - Contains point (x,y)
   - Has length-width ratio exactly a/b
   - Is as close as possible to (x,y) in terms of Euclidean distance to center
   - If still tied, choose lexicographically smallest (x1,y1,x2,y2)

## Key Insights

1. The ratio constraint: (x2-x1)/(y2-y1) = a/b
2. This means: b(x2-x1) = a(y2-y1)
3. We need to find integer solutions for x1,y1,x2,y2 such that:
   - 0 ≤ x1 ≤ x ≤ x2 ≤ n
   - 0 ≤ y1 ≤ y ≤ y2 ≤ m
   - b(x2-x1) = a(y2-y1)

## Mathematical Approach

From the ratio constraint: b(x2-x1) = a(y2-y1)

Let's define:
- width = x2 - x1
- height = y2 - y1

So: b × width = a × height, which means height = (b × width) / a

For height to be integer, width must be divisible by gcd(a,b). Let g = gcd(a,b).

Then width = k × (a/g), height = k × (b/g) for some positive integer k.

So we have:
- width = k × (a/g)
- height = k × (b/g)

## Finding Valid Rectangles

For a fixed k, we need:
- x1 ≤ x ≤ x1 + width ≤ n
- y1 ≤ y ≤ y1 + height ≤ m

This gives us constraints on x1 and y1:
- x1 ≥ x - width
- x1 ≤ x
- x1 ≥ 0
- x1 ≤ n - width

Similarly for y1:
- y1 ≥ y - height
- y1 ≤ y
- y1 ≥ 0
- y1 ≤ m - height

So x1 must be in [max(0, x - width), min(x, n - width)]
and y1 must be in [max(0, y - height), min(y, m - height)]

For each valid k, we can find the center and distance.

## Distance Calculation

Center of rectangle is at ((x1+x2)/2, (y1+y2)/2) = ((x1 + x1 + width)/2, (y1 + y1 + height)/2) = (x1 + width/2, y1 + height/2)

Distance to (x,y): √[(x1 + width/2 - x)² + (y1 + height/2 - y)²]

We want to minimize this distance, then lexicographically minimize (x1,y1,x2,y2).

## Algorithm

1. Compute g = gcd(a,b)
2. For each possible k, compute width and height
3. Find valid range for x1 and y1
4. Calculate center distance and find the minimum
5. Among all rectangles with minimum distance, choose lexicographically smallest

```python
import math
from math import gcd
from fractions import Fraction

def solve():
    n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())
    
    g = gcd(a, b)
    a_div_g = a // g
    b_div_g = b // g
    
    best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = None, None, None, None
    min_distance_squared = float('inf')
    
    # Find all possible values of k
    # width = k * a_div_g, height = k * b_div_g
    
    # Maximum possible k for width
    max_k_width = (n - 0) // a_div_g if a_div_g > 0 else 0
    # Maximum possible k for height  
    max_k_height = (m - 0) // b_div_g if b_div_g > 0 else 0
    
    max_k = min(max_k_width, max_k_height)
    
    # Try all possible k values
    for k in range(1, max_k + 1):
        width = k * a_div_g
        height = k * b_div_g
        
        # Check if we can place the rectangle such that it contains (x,y)
        # x1 must satisfy: x - width <= x1 <= x and 0 <= x1 <= n - width
        x1_min = max(0, x - width)
        x1_max = min(x, n - width)
        
        # y1 must satisfy: y - height <= y1 <= y and 0 <= y1 <= m - height
        y1_min = max(0, y - height)
        y1_max = min(y, m - height)
        
        # If valid range for x1 or y1, we can place the rectangle
        if x1_min <= x1_max and y1_min <= y1_max:
            # Try all possible combinations of x1, y1 in the valid ranges
            # We want to minimize distance to (x,y), then lexicographically smaller
            
            # For each valid x1 in range [x1_min, x1_max]
            for x1 in range(x1_min, x1_max + 1):
                x2 = x1 + width
                
                # For each valid y1 in range [y1_min, y1_max]
                for y1 in range(y1_min, y1_max + 1):
                    y2 = y1 + height
                    
                    # Calculate center
                    center_x = x1 + width / 2.0
                    center_y = y1 + height / 2.0
                    
                    # Calculate distance squared to (x,y)
                    dist_squared = (center_x - x) ** 2 + (center_y - y) ** 2
                    
                    # Check if this is better
                    if (dist_squared < min_distance_squared or 
                        (dist_squared == min_distance_squared and 
                         (best_x1 is None or 
                          (x1 < best_x1 or (x1 == best_x1 and y1 < best_y1) or 
                           (x1 == best_x1 and y1 == best_y1 and x2 < best_x2) or 
                           (x1 == best_x1 and y1 == best_y1 and x2 == best_x2 and y2 < best_y2)))):
                        
                        min_distance_squared = dist_squared
                        best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1, y1, x2, y2
    
    # If no solution found (should not happen given constraints), return some default
    if best_x1 is None:
        # Find the smallest valid rectangle
        k = 1
        width = a_div_g
        height = b_div_g
        
        x1_min = max(0, x - width)
        x1_max = min(x, n - width)
        
        y1_min = max(0, y - height)
        y1_max = min(y, m - height)
        
        if x1_min <= x1_max and y1_min <= y1_max:
            best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1_min, y1_min, x1_min + width, y1_min + height
    
    print(best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)

solve()
```

Wait, I notice a potential issue with the range of k. The maximum k might be too large, and we're trying exponentially many combinations. Let me optimize this.

Actually, I need to be more careful about the range of k. The constraint is that we need to contain (x,y), so:

1. x1 >= max(0, x - width)
2. x1 <= min(x, n - width)
3. So we need: max(0, x - width) <= min(x, n - width)

This means: x - width <= n - width, so x <= n (which is given)
And: x - width >= 0 or x - width <= x (always true)
Also: max(0, x - width) <= min(x, n - width)

Let me refine:

```python
import math
from math import gcd

n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())

g = gcd(a, b)
a_div_g = a // g
b_div_g = b // g

best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = None, None, None, None
min_distance_squared = float('inf')

# Find the maximum possible k
# width = k * a_div_g, height = k * b_div_g
# We need: x - width <= x <= x + width, so 0 <= x - width <= x
# and 0 <= y - height <= y
# and x + width <= n, y + height <= m

max_k = 0
# width <= n, height <= m
max_k = min(n // a_div_g, m // b_div_g) if a_div_g > 0 and b_div_g > 0 else 0

# But we also need to be able to contain (x,y)
# So x >= width and y >= height? No, we need x >= x1 and x <= x2 = x1+width
# x1 >= max(0, x - width) and x1 <= min(x, n - width)

for k in range(1, max_k + 1):
    width = k * a_div_g
    height = k * b_div_g
    
    # Check if we can place the rectangle to contain (x,y)
    # x1 must be in [max(0, x - width), min(x, n - width)]
    x1_min = max(0, x - width)
    x1_max = min(x, n - width)
    
    # y1 must be in [max(0, y - height), min(y, m - height)]
    y1_min = max(0, y - height)
    y1_max = min(y, m - height)
    
    # If valid ranges exist
    if x1_min <= x1_max and y1_min <= y1_max:
        # For each possible x1 and y1 in valid ranges
        for x1 in range(x1_min, x1_max + 1):
            x2 = x1 + width
            for y1 in range(y1_min, y1_max + 1):
                y2 = y1 + height
                
                # Calculate center
                center_x = x1 + width / 2.0
                center_y = y1 + height / 2.0
                
                # Calculate squared distance to (x,y)
                dist_squared = (center_x - x) ** 2 + (center_y - y) ** 2
                
                # Check if this is better
                if (dist_squared < min_distance_squared or 
                    (dist_squared == min_distance_squared and 
                     (best_x1 is None or 
                      (x1 < best_x1 or 
                       (x1 == best_x1 and y1 < best_y1) or 
                       (x1 == best_x1 and y1 == best_y1 and x2 < best_x2) or 
                       (x1 == best_x1 and y1 == best_y1 and x2 == best_x2 and y2 < best_y2))))):
                    
                    min_distance_squared = dist_squared
                    best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1, y1, x2, y2

# If no solution was found, find a valid one (shouldn't happen with given constraints)
if best_x1 is None:
    # Try k=1
    k = 1
    width = a_div_g
    height = b_div_g
    
    x1_min = max(0, x - width)
    x1_max = min(x, n - width)
    
    y1_min = max(0, y - height)
    y1_max = min(y, m - height)
    
    if x1_min <= x1_max and y1_min <= y1_max:
        best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1_min, y1_min, x1_min + width, y1_min + height

print(best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)
```

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You are given a rectangle grid. That grid's size is n × m. Let's denote the coordinate system on the grid. So, each point on the grid will have coordinates — a pair of integers (x, y) (0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m).

Your task is to find a maximum sub-rectangle on the grid (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2) so that it contains the given point (x, y), and its length-width ratio is exactly (a, b). In other words the following conditions must hold: 0 ≤ x_1 ≤ x ≤ x_2 ≤ n, 0 ≤ y_1 ≤ y ≤ y_2 ≤ m, $\frac{x_{2} - x_{1}}{y_{2} - y_{1}} = \frac{a}{b}$.

The sides of this sub-rectangle should be parallel to the axes. And values x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2 should be integers. [Image] 

If there are multiple solutions, find the rectangle which is closest to (x, y). Here "closest" means the Euclid distance between (x, y) and the center of the rectangle is as small as possible. If there are still multiple solutions, find the lexicographically minimum one. Here "lexicographically minimum" means that we should consider the sub-rectangle as sequence of integers (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2), so we can choose the lexicographically minimum one.


-----Input-----

The first line contains six integers n, m, x, y, a, b (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 10^9, 0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m, 1 ≤ a ≤ n, 1 ≤ b ≤ m).


-----Output-----

Print four integers x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2, which represent the founded sub-rectangle whose left-bottom point is (x_1, y_1) and right-up point is (x_2, y_2).


-----Examples-----
Input
9 9 5 5 2 1

Output
1 3 9 7

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find a sub-rectangle that:
1. Contains the point (x, y)
2. Has aspect ratio exactly a:b (width/height = a/b)
3. Is as close as possible to (x, y) in terms of Euclidean distance from the center
4. If still tied, choose lexicographically smallest (x1, y1, x2, y2)

## Key Insights

1. The aspect ratio constraint: (x2 - x1) / (y2 - y1) = a/b
2. This means: b * (x2 - x1) = a * (y2 - y1)
3. We can express width = w, height = h, so w/h = a/b → w = (a/b) * h
4. Since w and h must be integers, we need h to be divisible by b, and w = (a * h) / b

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Express variables
- Let width = w = k * a
- Let height = h = k * b
- For some positive integer k

### Step 2: Constraints on coordinates
- x1 ≤ x ≤ x2 → x1 ≤ x ≤ x1 + w → x - w ≤ x1 ≤ x
- y1 ≤ y ≤ y2 → y - h ≤ y1 ≤ y  
- x1 ≥ 0, x2 ≤ n → 0 ≤ x1 ≤ n - w
- y1 ≥ 0, y2 ≤ m → 0 ≤ y1 ≤ m - h

### Step 3: Find valid k values
For each k, we have:
- w = k * a
- h = k * b

Constraints on k:
- w ≥ 1, h ≥ 1 → k ≥ 1
- w ≤ n, h ≤ m → k * a ≤ n, k * b ≤ m → k ≤ min(n//a, m//b)

### Step 4: Find valid rectangles
For each valid k, find the range of x1 and y1:
- x1 ∈ [max(0, x - w), min(x, n - w)]
- y1 ∈ [max(0, y - h), min(y, m - h)]

### Step 5: Choose optimal solution
- Among valid rectangles, find one with minimum Euclidean distance from center
- Center of rectangle is at ((x1+x2)/2, (y1+y2)/2) = ((x1 + x1 + w)/2, (y1 + y1 + h)/2) = (x1 + w/2, y1 + h/2)
- Distance = sqrt((x - (x1 + w/2))² + (y - (y1 + h/2))²)

### Step 6: Handle ties
If multiple rectangles have same distance, choose lexicographically smallest.

## Implementation

```python
import math

def solve():
    n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())
    
    # We need to find k such that:
    # width = k * a, height = k * b
    # Constraints: k >= 1, k * a <= n, k * b <= m
    # So k <= min(n // a, m // b)
    
    max_k = min(n // a, m // b)
    best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = None, None, None, None
    best_distance_squared = float('inf')
    
    # Try all possible values of k from 1 to max_k
    for k in range(1, max_k + 1):
        width = k * a
        height = k * b
        
        # Calculate valid ranges for x1 and y1
        # x1 should satisfy: x1 <= x <= x1 + width
        # So: x - width <= x1 <= x
        # And x1 >= 0, x1 <= n - width
        x1_min = max(0, x - width)
        x1_max = min(x, n - width)
        
        # y1 should satisfy: y1 <= y <= y1 + height  
        # So: y - height <= y1 <= y
        # And y1 >= 0, y1 <= m - height
        y1_min = max(0, y - height)
        y1_max = min(y, m - height)
        
        # If no valid range, skip this k
        if x1_min > x1_max or y1_min > y1_max:
            continue
            
        # For each valid (x1, y1), calculate center and distance
        # Center is at (x1 + width/2, y1 + height/2)
        # Distance squared from (x, y) is:
        # (x - (x1 + width/2))² + (y - (y1 + height/2))²
        # = (x - x1 - width/2)² + (y - y1 - height/2)²
        
        # We can iterate through valid x1 and y1 values
        for x1 in range(x1_min, x1_max + 1):
            for y1 in range(y1_min, y1_max + 1):
                x2 = x1 + width
                y2 = y1 + height
                
                # Calculate distance squared from center to (x, y)
                center_x = x1 + width / 2.0
                center_y = y1 + height / 2.0
                distance_squared = (x - center_x) ** 2 + (y - center_y) ** 2
                
                # Check if this is better than current best
                if (distance_squared < best_distance_squared or 
                    (distance_squared == best_distance_squared and 
                     (best_x1 is None or 
                      (x1 < best_x1 or (x1 == best_x1 and y1 < best_y1) or 
                       (x1 == best_x1 and y1 == best_y1 and x2 < best_x2) or 
                       (x1 == best_x1 and y1 == best_y1 and x2 == best_x2 and y2 < best_y2))))):
                    
                    best_distance_squared = distance_squared
                    best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1, y1, x2, y2
    
    print(best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)

solve()
```

Wait, this solution has a performance issue because the ranges for x1 and y1 could be very large (up to 10^9). We need to optimize this.

Let me reconsider. The key insight is that we want the center to be as close as possible to (x, y). The center is at (x1 + width/2, y1 + height/2).

For a fixed k, we want to minimize:
(x - (x1 + width/2))² + (y - (y1 + height/2))²

Which is equivalent to minimizing:
(x - x1 - width/2)² + (y - y1 - height/2)²

For each k, we can find the optimal x1 and y1 by considering the ranges.

```python
n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())

# Find maximum possible k
max_k = min(n // a, m // b)
best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = None, None, None, None
best_distance_squared = float('inf')

# Try all possible values of k
for k in range(1, max_k + 1):
    width = k * a
    height = k * b
    
    # Valid ranges for x1 and y1
    x1_min = max(0, x - width)
    x1_max = min(x, n - width)
    
    y1_min = max(0, y - height)
    y1_max = min(y, m - height)
    
    if x1_min > x1_max or y1_min > y1_max:
        continue
    
    # For this k, find optimal x1 and y1
    # The center should be as close as possible to (x, y)
    # Center = (x1 + width/2, y1 + height/2)
    # We want to minimize distance to (x, y)
    
    # Optimal x1: should be as close as possible to x - width/2
    x1_optimal = x - width / 2.0
    x1_candidate = int(x1_optimal)
    
    # Check the boundaries and the optimal point
    candidates_x1 = []
    if x1_min <= x1_max:
        candidates_x1.append(x1_min)
        candidates_x1.append(x1_max)
        if x1_min <= x1_optimal <= x1_max:
            candidates_x1.append(int(x1_optimal))
            candidates_x1.append(int(x1_optimal) + 1)
            candidates_x1.append(int(x1_optimal) - 1)
    
    candidates_x1 = [c for c in candidates_x1 if x1_min <= c <= x1_max]
    
    candidates_y1 = []
    if y1_min <= y1_max:
        candidates_y1.append(y1_min)
        candidates_y1.append(y1_max)
        if y1_min <= y1_optimal <= y1_max:
            candidates_y1.append(int(y1_optimal))
            candidates_y1.append(int(y1_optimal) + 1)
            candidates_y1.append(int(y1_optimal) - 1)
    
    candidates_y1 = [c for c in candidates_y1 if y1_min <= c <= y1_max]
    
    # Check all candidate pairs
    for x1 in candidates_x1:
        for y1 in candidates_y1:
            x2 = x1 + width
            y2 = y1 + height
            
            # Calculate distance squared from center to (x, y)
            center_x = x1 + width / 2.0
            center_y = y1 + height / 2.0
            distance_squared = (x - center_x) ** 2 + (y - center_y) ** 2
            
            # Update best solution
            if (distance_squared < best_distance_squared or 
                (distance_squared == best_distance_squared and 
                 (best_x1 is None or 
                  (x1 < best_x1 or 
                   (x1 == best_x1 and y1 < best_y1) or 
                   (x1 == best_x1 and y1 == best_y1 and x2 < best_x2) or 
                   (x1 == best_x1 and y1 == best_y1 and x2 == best_x2 and y2 < best_y2))))):
                
                best_distance_squared = distance_squared
                best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1, y1, x2, y2
```

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Ma5termind and Subway are bored of their normal life.They want to do something interesting so that they can enjoy their last sem of college life.As usual Ma5termind comes up with a simple and interesting game.

Ma5termind gives Subway a compressed string. A compressed String is composed of characters and numbers.Every character in a compressed string contains a number. Ex a3b1c2 it represents aaabcc. Other compressed string could be a22b2a1 , z5c2x36. Each number in the compressed string indicates the number of times the corresponding character occurs in the string. The final string is 1 indexed.  Ma5termind wants Subway to sort the compressed string and tell him the kth character in the sorted compressed string.Since subway got speed Ma5termind asks him various such questions.

Help Subway in answering Ma5termind's question.

Input:

The first line contains the compressed string.This is followed by Q that indicates the number of questions Ma5termind shoots on Subway.Follows Q lines each line contain an integer K.

Output:

For every question output the Kth  character if it exists else print -1 .

Constraints:

2 ≤ Length of Compressed String ≤ 10^4

1 ≤ Length of Final String ≤ 10^18

1 ≤ Q ≤ 10^5

1 ≤ K ≤ 10^18

Every character in the string is lowercase English letter.

Note:

The number for a character may contain leading zeroes.

SAMPLE OUTPUT

b

a

c

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a compressed string like "a2b3c2a1" which expands to "aaabbbcc" (a appears 2+1=3 times, b appears 3 times, c appears 2 times)
2. We need to sort the final string alphabetically
3. For Q queries, we need to find the Kth character in the sorted string
4. If K is out of bounds, return -1

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the compressed string
- Extract characters and their frequencies
- Handle numbers with multiple digits (like "22" or "36")

### Step 2: Build frequency map
- For each character, count how many times it appears in the final string
- Store this in a dictionary or list

### Step 3: Sort characters
- Sort characters alphabetically (a-z)

### Step 4: Answer queries efficiently
- Since K can be up to 10^18, we can't generate the entire string
- We need to find the Kth character by calculating cumulative frequencies
- For each character in sorted order, check if K falls within its range

### Step 5: Implementation approach
1. Parse the input string to get character frequencies
2. Sort characters by their ASCII value
3. For each query K, iterate through characters and find which character contains the Kth position

```python
def parse_compressed_string(s):
    """Parse compressed string and return character frequencies"""
    freq = {}
    i = 0
    n = len(s)
    
    while i < n:
        char = s[i]
        i += 1
        
        # Extract number (can have multiple digits)
        num_str = ""
        while i < n and s[i].isdigit():
            num_str += s[i]
            i += 1
        
        # Convert number to integer
        count = int(num_str) if num_str else 0
        
        # Update frequency
        if char not in freq:
            freq[char] = 0
        freq[char] += count
    
    return freq

def solve():
    # Read input
    compressed_str = input().strip()
    q = int(input().strip())
    
    # Parse compressed string to get frequencies
    freq = parse_compressed_string(compressed_str)
    
    # Sort characters alphabetically
    sorted_chars = sorted(freq.keys())
    
    # Convert to list of (character, frequency) tuples
    char_freq_list = [(char, freq[char]) for char in sorted_chars]
    
    # Process queries
    for _ in range(q):
        k = int(input().strip())
        
        # Check if k is within valid range
        total_length = sum(freq[char] for char in freq)
        if k > total_length:
            print(-1)
            continue
            
        # Find which character contains the kth position
        current_pos = 0
        for char, count in char_freq_list:
            if current_pos + count >= k:
                # k falls within this character's range
                print(char)
                break
            current_pos += count
        else:
            # This should not happen since we checked total_length
            print(-1)

solve()
```

## Explanation of the Algorithm

1. **Parsing**: We traverse the compressed string character by character, extracting digits to form the count for each character.

2. **Frequency Mapping**: We build a dictionary to store how many times each character appears in the final string.

3. **Sorting**: Characters are sorted alphabetically to ensure the final string is in correct order.

4. **Query Processing**: For each query K:
   - First check if K exceeds the total length of the final string
   - Then iterate through characters in sorted order, keeping track of cumulative positions
   - When we find a character whose cumulative range contains K, that's our answer

## Time Complexity
- Parsing: O(n) where n is length of compressed string
- Sorting: O(c log c) where c is number of unique characters (c ≤ 26)
- Each query: O(c) where c is number of unique characters
- Total: O(n + Q * c) which is efficient given constraints

## Space Complexity
- O(c) for storing character frequencies and sorted list

The solution efficiently handles large values of K (up to 10^18) by avoiding string generation and using mathematical computation to find the Kth character directly.

def parse_compressed_string(s):
    """Parse compressed string and return character frequencies"""
    freq = {}
    i = 0
    n = len(s)
    
    while i < n:
        char = s[i]
        i += 1
        
        # Extract number (can have multiple digits)
        num_str = ""
        while i < n and s[i].isdigit():
            num_str += s[i]
            i += 1
        
        # Convert number to integer
        count = int(num_str) if num_str else 0
        
        # Update frequency
        if char not in freq:
            freq[char] = 0
        freq[char] += count
    
    return freq

def solve():
    # Read input
    compressed_str = input().strip()
    q = int(input().strip())
    
    # Parse compressed string to get frequencies
    freq = parse_compressed_string(compressed_str)
    
    # Sort characters alphabetically
    sorted_chars = sorted(freq.keys())
    
    # Convert to list of (character, frequency) tuples
    char_freq_list = [(char, freq[char]) for char in sorted_chars]
    
    # Process queries
    for _ in range(q):
        k = int(input().strip())
        
        # Check if k is within valid range
        total_length = sum(freq[char] for char in freq)
        if k > total_length:
            print(-1)
            continue
            
        # Find which character contains the kth position
        current_pos = 0
        for char, count in char_freq_list:
            if current_pos + count >= k:
                # k falls within this character's range
                print(char)
                break
            current_pos += count

            # This should not happen since we checked total_length
            print(-1)

Ma5termind and Subway are bored of their normal life.They want to do something interesting so that they can enjoy their last sem of college life.As usual Ma5termind comes up with a simple and interesting game.

Ma5termind gives Subway a compressed string. A compressed String is composed of characters and numbers.Every character in a compressed string contains a number. Ex a3b1c2 it represents aaabcc. Other compressed string could be a22b2a1 , z5c2x36. Each number in the compressed string indicates the number of times the corresponding character occurs in the string. The final string is 1 indexed.  Ma5termind wants Subway to sort the compressed string and tell him the kth character in the sorted compressed string.Since subway got speed Ma5termind asks him various such questions.

Help Subway in answering Ma5termind's question.

Input:

The first line contains the compressed string.This is followed by Q that indicates the number of questions Ma5termind shoots on Subway.Follows Q lines each line contain an integer K.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a compressed string like "a2b3c2a1" which represents "aaabbbcc"
2. We need to sort the characters in the final string and answer Q queries about the Kth character
3. The final string is sorted lexicographically (alphabetically)
4. If K is out of bounds, return -1

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the compressed string
- We need to extract characters and their frequencies
- Handle numbers that might have multiple digits (like "22")

### Step 2: Build the final string
- Create a dictionary to store character frequencies
- For each character-number pair, add the character count to the frequency map

### Step 3: Sort characters
- Sort characters alphabetically (a to z)

### Step 4: Answer queries efficiently
- Since the final string can be very long (up to 10^18), we cannot build the entire string
- We need to answer queries by calculating the position directly
- For each query K, find which character it corresponds to by checking cumulative counts

### Step 5: Implementation details
- Use a list of (character, frequency) pairs and sort by character
- For each query K, iterate through sorted characters and check if K falls within the current character's frequency range
- If K is greater than the total length, return -1

```python
def solve():
    compressed = input().strip()
    q = int(input().strip())
    
    # Parse the compressed string to get character frequencies
    char_freq = {}
    i = 0
    n = len(compressed)
    
    while i < n:
        char = compressed[i]
        i += 1
        
        # Extract the number following the character
        num_str = ""
        while i < n and compressed[i].isdigit():
            num_str += compressed[i]
            i += 1
        
        # Parse the number (can have multiple digits)
        count = int(num_str)
        
        # Add to frequency map
        if char in char_freq:
            char_freq[char] += count
        else:
            char_freq[char] = count
    
    # Create list of (character, frequency) pairs and sort by character
    char_freq_list = sorted(char_freq.items())
    
    # Process queries
    for _ in range(q):
        k = int(input().strip())
        
        # Check if k is within valid range
        total_length = sum(char_freq_list[i][1] for i in range(len(char_freq_list)))
        
        if k > total_length:
            print(-1)
        else:
            # Find which character corresponds to position k
            current_pos = 0
            for char, freq in char_freq_list:
                if current_pos + freq >= k:
                    # K falls within this character's range
                    print(char)
                    break
                current_pos += freq
            else:
                # This shouldn't happen due to our range check
                print(-1)

solve()
```

## Explanation of Algorithm:

1. **Parse Compression**: Iterate through the string, extract each character and its frequency (handling multi-digit numbers)

2. **Build Frequency Map**: Store character frequencies in a dictionary

3. **Sort Characters**: Sort the characters alphabetically so that we can process them in order

4. **Answer Queries**: For each query K:
   - Calculate total length of the final string
   - If K exceeds total length, return -1
   - Otherwise, iterate through sorted characters and find which character's range contains position K

5. **Efficiency**: Time complexity is O(n + q * m) where n is compressed string length, q is number of queries, and m is number of unique characters. Since we iterate through at most 26 unique characters for each query, this is efficient even for large K values.

The solution handles edge cases like leading zeros in numbers and ensures we don't exceed the bounds of the final string.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a compressed string like "a2b3c2a1" which represents "aaabbbcc"
2. We need to sort the final string lexicographically (alphabetically)
3. For each query K, we need to find the K-th character in the sorted string
4. If K is out of bounds, return -1

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the compressed string
- We need to extract characters and their repetition counts
- Handle numbers with leading zeros (like "22" or "36")

### Step 2: Build the final string
- Create a list of (character, count) pairs
- Sort by character (lexicographically)

### Step 3: Answer queries efficiently
- Since the final string can be very long (up to 10^18 characters), we can't build the entire string
- Instead, we need to determine which character the K-th position belongs to
- For each character, we calculate the cumulative count of characters that come before it
- If K falls within a character's range, return that character

### Step 4: Handle edge cases
- K might be greater than the total length of the string
- We need to check if K is valid

## Implementation

```python
def solve():
    compressed = input().strip()
    q = int(input().strip())
    
    # Parse the compressed string to get (char, count) pairs
    pairs = []
    i = 0
    while i < len(compressed):
        char = compressed[i]
        i += 1
        
        # Parse the number that follows
        num_str = ""
        while i < len(compressed) and compressed[i].isdigit():
            num_str += compressed[i]
            i += 1
        
        count = int(num_str) if num_str else 0
        pairs.append((char, count))
    
    # Sort pairs lexicographically by character
    pairs.sort()
    
    # Calculate total length of the final string
    total_length = sum(count for _, count in pairs)
    
    # Process each query
    for _ in range(q):
        k = int(input().strip())
        
        # Check if K is valid
        if k > total_length:
            print(-1)
            continue
            
        # Find which character corresponds to the k-th position
        current_pos = 0
        
        for char, count in pairs:
            if current_pos + count >= k:
                # K falls within this character's range
                print(char)
                break
            current_pos += count
        else:
            # This should not happen given our bounds checking
            print(-1)

solve()
```

   - a: 2

   - After combining: a: 3, b: 3, c: 2

4. Queries:
   - K=2: current_pos=0, a has 3 which >= 2, so answer is 'a'
   - K=5: current_pos=0, a (0+3) < 5, so current_pos=3. Then b has 3 which >= (5-3)=2, so answer is 'b'
   - K=1: current_pos=0, a has 3 >= 1, so answer is 'a'
   - K=8: current_pos=0, a (0+3)<8, current_pos=3; b (3+3=6)<8, current_pos=6; c has 2 >= (8-6)=2, so answer is 'c'

## Problem Understanding

2. We need to sort the final string and answer queries about the Kth character
3. The final string is sorted lexicographically (alphabetically)
4. If K is out of bounds, return -1

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the compressed string
- Extract characters and their frequencies
- Handle numbers that might have multiple digits (like "22")

### Step 2: Build the final string
- For each character, create a list of that many characters
- Combine all characters and sort them

### Step 3: Answer queries
- For each query K, check if K is within the valid range
- Return the Kth character (1-indexed)

### Step 4: Optimization
Since the final string length can be up to 10^18, we can't actually build the string. We need to:
1. Calculate the total length of the final string
2. For each query, find which character corresponds to position K

## Implementation Strategy

1. Parse the compressed string to get character frequencies
2. Sort the characters by their frequency
3. For each query K:
   - Check if K exceeds total length
   - Find which character the Kth position belongs to
   - Return that character

```python
def solve():
    compressed = input().strip()
    q = int(input().strip())
    
    # Parse the compressed string to get character frequencies
    char_freq = {}
    i = 0
    n = len(compressed)
    
    while i < n:
        char = compressed[i]
        i += 1
        
        # Extract the number (digits)
        num_str = ""
        while i < n and compressed[i].isdigit():
            num_str += compressed[i]
            i += 1
        
        # Convert to integer
        count = int(num_str) if num_str else 0
        char_freq[char] = char_freq.get(char, 0) + count
    
    # Calculate total length of final string
    total_length = sum(char_freq.values())
    
    # Process queries
    for _ in range(q):
        k = int(input().strip())
        
        # Check if k is valid
        if k > total_length:
            print(-1)
        else:
            # Find which character is at position k
            # Sort characters by their frequency (we need to maintain order)
            sorted_chars = sorted(char_freq.keys())
            
            current_pos = 0
            for char in sorted_chars:
                freq = char_freq[char]
                if current_pos + freq >= k:
                    # k falls within this character's range
                    print(char)
                    break
                current_pos += freq
            else:
                # This shouldn't happen if k is valid
                print(-1)
```

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a compressed string like "a2b3c2a1" which represents "aaabbbcc"
2. We need to decompress the string by repeating each character according to its count
3. Then sort the resulting string (lexicographically)
4. For Q queries, we need to find the Kth character in the sorted string
5. If K is out of bounds, return -1

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the compressed string
- We need to extract characters and their frequencies
- Handle numbers with leading zeros (like "01" or "22")

### Step 2: Generate the final string
- For each character and its count, create that many copies
- Sort the characters alphabetically (since we want lexicographical order)

### Step 3: Handle queries efficiently
- Since the final string can be very long (up to 10^18), we can't actually build the string
- Instead, we can calculate the position by summing up the frequencies until we reach or exceed K
- Use prefix sums to find which character corresponds to position K

### Step 4: Implementation approach
1. Parse the input string to get character frequencies
2. Sort the characters by their frequency (to maintain lexicographical order)
3. For each query K, find which character contains the Kth position using binary search or prefix sum

## Code Implementation

```python
def solve():
    compressed = input().strip()
    q = int(input().strip())
    
    # Parse the compressed string to get character frequencies
    char_freq = {}
    i = 0
    n = len(compressed)
    
    while i < n:
        char = compressed[i]
        i += 1
        
        # Extract the number (handle multi-digit numbers)
        num_str = ""
        while i < n and compressed[i].isdigit():
            num_str += compressed[i]
            i += 1
        
        # Convert to integer
        count = int(num_str) if num_str else 0
        if count > 0:
            char_freq[char] = char_freq.get(char, 0) + count
    
    # Sort characters alphabetically (lexicographical order)
    sorted_chars = sorted(char_freq.keys())
    
    # Create prefix sums for efficient queries
    prefix_sum = []
    current_sum = 0
    
    for char in sorted_chars:
        count = char_freq[char]
        current_sum += count
        prefix_sum.append((char, current_sum))
    
    # Process queries
    for _ in range(q):
        k = int(input().strip())
        
        # Check if k is within bounds
        if k > prefix_sum[-1][1]:
            print(-1)
        else:
            # Find the character where k falls
            for char, total_count in prefix_sum:
                if k <= total_count:
                    print(char)
                    break

solve()
```

## How it works:

1. **Parsing**: We traverse the compressed string character by character, extracting numbers when we encounter digits.

2. **Frequency counting**: We build a dictionary to store how many times each character appears.

3. **Sorting**: We sort the characters alphabetically since the final string should be sorted lexicographically.

4. **Prefix sums**: We create a list of tuples (character, cumulative_count) where cumulative_count is the total number of characters up to that point.

5. **Query processing**: For each query K:
   - If K exceeds the total length, return -1
   - Otherwise, find the first character in our sorted list where the cumulative count is >= K
   - This character is the Kth character in the final sorted string

## Time Complexity:
- Parsing: O(n) where n is length of compressed string
- Sorting: O(m log m) where m is number of unique characters (m ≤ 26)
- Queries: O(Q log m) due to binary search (though we're using linear scan which is fine since m is small)

## Space Complexity:
- O(m) for storing character frequencies and prefix sums

The solution efficiently handles the large constraint of up to 10^18 for K by avoiding building the actual string.

Submitted Code

View Extracted Code 
def solve():
    compressed = input().strip()
    q = int(input().strip())
    
    # Parse the compressed string to get character frequencies
    char_freq = {}
    i = 0
    n = len(compressed)
    
    while i < n:
        char = compressed[i]
        i += 1
        
        # Extract the number (handle multi-digit numbers)
        num_str = ""
        while i < n and compressed[i].isdigit():
            num_str += compressed[i]
            i += 1
        
        # Convert to integer
        count = int(num_str) if num_str else 0
        if count > 0:
            char_freq[char] = char_freq.get(char, 0) + count
    
    # Sort characters alphabetically (lexicographical order)
    sorted_chars = sorted(char_freq.keys())
    
    # Create prefix sums for efficient queries
    prefix_sum = []
    current_sum = 0
    
    for char in sorted_chars:
        count = char_freq[char]
        current_sum += count
        prefix_sum.append((char, current_sum))
    
    # Process queries
    for _ in range(q):
        k = int(input().strip())
        
        # Check if k is within bounds
        if k > prefix_sum[-1][1]:
            print(-1)
        else:
            # Find the character where k falls
            for char, total_count in prefix_sum:
                if k <= total_count:
                    print(char)
                    break

solve()

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a compressed string like "a2b3c2a1" which represents "aaabbbcc"
2. We need to sort the characters in the final string (which is already sorted by character, but we need to handle the frequency properly)
3. For each query K, we need to find the K-th character in the sorted final string
4. If K is beyond the length of the final string, return -1

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the compressed string
- Extract characters and their frequencies
- Handle numbers with leading zeros (like "22" or "36")

### Step 2: Create frequency map
- Go through the compressed string and for each character, extract the number that follows
- Store the frequency of each character

### Step 3: Sort characters
- Since we want the K-th character in sorted order, we need to sort characters in alphabetical order

### Step 4: Answer queries efficiently
- Instead of building the entire string (which could be 10^18 characters), we need to answer queries in O(1) or O(log n) time
- For each query K, we traverse the sorted characters and accumulate frequencies until we reach or pass K

## Implementation

```python
def solve():
    # Read the compressed string
    compressed = input().strip()
    
    # Read number of queries
    q = int(input().strip())
    
    # Parse the compressed string to get character frequencies
    freq_map = {}
    i = 0
    n = len(compressed)
    
    while i < n:
        char = compressed[i]
        i += 1
        
        # Extract the number that follows the character
        num_str = ""
        while i < n and compressed[i].isdigit():
            num_str += compressed[i]
            i += 1
        
        # Convert number string to integer
        num = int(num_str) if num_str else 0
        
        # Add to frequency map
        if char in freq_map:
            freq_map[char] += num
        else:
            freq_map[char] = num
    
    # Convert frequency map to sorted list of (character, frequency)
    sorted_chars = sorted(freq_map.items())
    
    # Precompute prefix sums for efficient querying
    prefix_sum = []
    current_sum = 0
    
    for char, freq in sorted_chars:
        current_sum += freq
        prefix_sum.append(current_sum)
    
    # Answer queries
    for _ in range(q):
        k = int(input().strip())
        
        # If k is greater than total length, return -1
        total_length = prefix_sum[-1] if prefix_sum else 0
        if k > total_length:
            print(-1)
        else:
            # Find which character contains the k-th position
            # Find the first prefix sum >= k
            left, right = 0, len(prefix_sum) - 1
            result_char = None
            
            while left <= right:
                mid = (left + right) // 2
                if prefix_sum[mid] >= k:
                    result_char = sorted_chars[mid][0]
                    right = mid - 1
                else:
                    left = mid + 1
            
            print(result_char)

solve()
```

## How it works:

1. **Parsing**: I iterate through the compressed string, extracting each character followed by its frequency number (which can have leading zeros).

2. **Frequency Map**: Store each character with its total frequency.

3. **Sorting**: Sort characters alphabetically since we want the final string to be sorted.

4. **Prefix Sum**: Create a prefix sum array where each element represents the cumulative frequency up to that character.

5. **Query Processing**: For each query K:
   - If K > total length, return -1
   - Otherwise, use binary search to find which character contains the K-th position

## Time Complexity:
- O(n + m log m) for parsing and sorting where n is length of compressed string and m is number of unique characters
- O(log m) per query (binary search)

## Space Complexity:
- O(m) for storing the frequency map and prefix sums

The solution handles the large constraints efficiently since we're not building the actual final string (which could be up to 10^18 characters), but rather using mathematical operations to find the K-th character.

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a compressed string like "a2b3c2a1" which represents "aaabbbcc"
2. Each character is followed by a number indicating how many times it appears
3. We need to sort the final string lexicographically (alphabetically)
4. For Q queries, we need to find the Kth character in the sorted string
5. If K is out of bounds, return -1

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the compressed string
- Iterate through the string and extract character-groups
- For each character, extract the number that follows it (which might have leading zeros)
- Store the character and its frequency

### Step 2: Sort the character frequencies
- Sort by character (alphabetically) since we need lexicographic order
- This gives us the sorted final string

### Step 3: Answer each query
- For each query K, we need to check if K is within the valid range
- We need to find which character is at position K in the sorted string
- We can do this by maintaining a running sum of character counts

### Step 4: Handle edge cases
- K might be larger than the total length of the final string
- Need to return -1 if K is out of bounds

## Implementation

```python
def solve():
    compressed = input().strip()
    q = int(input().strip())
    
    # Parse the compressed string to get character frequencies
    char_freq = {}
    i = 0
    n = len(compressed)
    
    while i < n:
        char = compressed[i]
        i += 1
        
        # Extract the number that follows the character
        num_str = ""
        while i < n and compressed[i].isdigit():
            num_str += compressed[i]
            i += 1
        
        # Convert to integer
        count = int(num_str) if num_str else 0
        char_freq[char] = char_freq.get(char, 0) + count
    
    # Create sorted list of (character, count) pairs
    sorted_chars = sorted(char_freq.items())
    
    # Calculate total length of final string
    total_length = sum(count for _, count in sorted_chars)
    
    # Answer each query
    for _ in range(q):
        k = int(input().strip())
        
        # Check if K is valid
        if k > total_length:
            print(-1)
        else:
            # Find which character is at position k
            cumulative_count = 0
            for char, count in sorted_chars:
                cumulative_count += count
                if k <= cumulative_count:
                    print(char)
                    break

solve()
```

## How it works with the example:

Input: "a2b3c2a1", Q=4, queries: 2, 5, 1, 8

1. Parse "a2b3c2a1":
   - a followed by 2 → count = 2
   - b followed by 3 → count = 3  
   - c followed by 2 → count = 2
   - a followed by 1 → count = 1
   - Total a count = 2 + 1 = 3
   - Total b count = 3
   - Total c count = 2

2. Sorted by character: [('a', 3), ('b', 3), ('c', 2)]
   - Total length = 3 + 3 + 2 = 8
   - Final string: "aaabbbcc"

3. Queries:
   - K=2: Position 2 is 'a' → output 'a'
   - K=5: Position 5 is 'b' → output 'b'  
   - K=1: Position 1 is 'a' → output 'a'
   - K=8: Position 8 is 'c' → output 'c'

The solution efficiently handles large K values by using prefix sums and avoids constructing the entire string in memory.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a compressed string like "a2b3c2a1" which expands to "aaabbbcc"
2. We need to sort the final string (lexicographically) and answer Q queries about the Kth character
3. If K is out of bounds, return -1

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the compressed string
- Extract characters and their frequencies
- Handle numbers with leading zeros (like "22" or "36")

### Step 2: Generate the sorted string
- Sort characters lexicographically
- Calculate total length by summing up all frequencies

### Step 3: Answer queries efficiently
- Since the total length can be up to 10^18, we can't generate the entire string
- Instead, we need to find which character at position K belongs to

### Step 4: Algorithm for finding Kth character
1. For each character in lexicographic order (a, b, c, ...):
   - Calculate how many characters would come before this character
   - If K falls within this range, return the current character
   - Otherwise, subtract the count from K and move to the next character

### Step 5: Implementation

```python
def solve():
    compressed = input().strip()
    q = int(input().strip())
    
    # Parse the compressed string to get (char, count) pairs
    chars = []
    i = 0
    n = len(compressed)
    
    while i < n:
        char = compressed[i]
        i += 1
        
        # Extract the number part
        num_str = ""
        while i < n and compressed[i].isdigit():
            num_str += compressed[i]
            i += 1
        
        count = int(num_str) if num_str else 0
        chars.append((char, count))
    
    # Sort by character (lexicographically)
    chars.sort()
    
    # Process queries
    for _ in range(q):
        k = int(input().strip())
        
        # Check if k is within valid range
        total_length = sum(count for _, count in chars)
        if k > total_length:
            print(-1)
            continue
            
        # Find which character is at position k
        current_pos = 0
        for char, count in chars:
            if current_pos + count >= k:
                # k falls within this character's range
                print(char)
                break
            current_pos += count
        else:
            # This should not happen due to the above check
            print(-1)

solve()
```

## Explanation of the Algorithm

1. **Parse**: Extract each character and its frequency by iterating through the string
2. **Sort**: Sort the character-frequency pairs lexicographically (a, b, c, ...)
3. **Query processing**: For each query K:
   - Check if K is within the total length
   - Iterate through sorted characters, keeping track of cumulative position
   - When K falls within the current character's range, output that character
   - If we reach the end without finding a match, output -1 (though this should not occur with valid input)

## Time Complexity
- Parsing: O(n) where n is length of compressed string
- Sorting: O(m log m) where m is number of unique characters (m ≤ 26)
- Each query: O(m) where m ≤ 26
- Overall: O(n + Q × m) which is efficient for given constraints

## Space Complexity
- O(m) for storing character-frequency pairs

The solution handles the large constraint of up to 10^18 total length by not building the actual string, just finding the Kth character through mathematical positioning.

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

A binary string of length N is a string of N characters, where each character is either 0 or 1. 

Wet Shark makes a list binStrings, consisting of all 2^N N-digit binary strings. For each string X in binStrings, Wet Shark runs a function ZeroShark(X), defined as follows (psuedocode):

bool ZeroShark(x):
//note the zero indexing
n = length(x)
counter = 0
FOR i = 1 to n-1
    IF x[i] == '0' and x[i - 1] == '0'
        INCREMENT counter
        END IF
ENDFOR
return (counter == 1)

Wet Shark stores an element X in a new list, binTrues  if ZeroShark(X) returns True for that X . In otherwords, binTrues consists of all the elements X in binStrings such that ZeroShark(X) returns True.

Given T testcases, where each testcase consists of integer N, calculate the length of binTrues for each N. Because the lists can get large, output your answer modulo 10^9+7=1000000007

INPUT

The first line of the input contains a single integer T, the number of such N.

The next T lines of the input contain a single integer N, as described in the problem.

OUTPUT

Output T separate lines, each containing the number of elements in the resulting list binTrues for each N in the input. The lists may be large, so output the answer modulo 10^9+7=1000000007

CONSTRAINTS

1 ≤ T ≤ 10

1 ≤ N ≤ 10^5

SAMPLE INPUT
2

2

3

SAMPLE OUTPUT
1

2

Explanation

When N=2, the list binStrings is equal to ['00', '01', '10', '11'].

Calling ZeroShark on each element in binStrings, the function only returns true when X is equal to 00. When X = 01, 10, 11, the counter in ZeroShark is equal to 0. 

Therefore, binTrues is equal to ['00'], and has a length of 1.

When N=3, the list binStrings is equal to ['000', '001', '010', '100', '011', '101', '110', '111'].

Calling ZeroShark on each element in binStrings, the function only returns true when X is equal to 001 or 100.

Perfect! The formula works.

So the answer for a given N is the sum from i=0 to N-2 of fib[i] * fib[N-i-2], where fib[k] = 1 for k=0, 1 for k=1, and fib[k] = fib[k-1] + fib[k-2] for k≥2.

But we need to handle edge cases:
- If N < 2, then no "00" can exist, so 0

For N=1: no positions where i ≤ N-2 = -1, so sum is 0

Wait, N=1: i from 0 to -1 → no terms → sum = 0 ✓

So algorithm:
1. Precompute Fibonacci numbers up to 10^5
2. For each query N:
   - If N < 2: answer is 0
   - Else: answer = sum_{i=0}^{N-2} fib[i] * fib[N-i-2] mod (10^9+7)

```python
def solve():
    MOD = 1000000007
    
    # Precompute Fibonacci numbers up to 10^5
    max_n = 100000
    fib = [0] * (max_n + 1)
    if max_n >= 0:
        fib[0] = 1
    if max_n >= 1:
        fib[1] = 1
    for i in range(2, max_n + 1):
        fib[i] = (fib[i-1] + fib[i-2]) % MOD
    
    T = int(input())
    for _ in range(T):
        N = int(input())
        if N < 2:
            print(0)
        else:
            total = 0
            # Sum over i from 0 to N-2
            for i in range(0, N - 1):
                # left part: length i, count = fib[i]
                # right part: length N-i-2, count = fib[N-i-2]
                right_len = N - i - 2
                if right_len >= 0:
                    term = (fib[i] * fib[right_len]) % MOD
                    total = (total + term) % MOD
            print(total)

solve()
```

def solve():
    MOD = 1000000007
    
    # Precompute Fibonacci numbers up to 10^5
    max_n = 100000
    fib = [0] * (max_n + 1)
    if max_n >= 0:
        fib[0] = 1
    if max_n >= 1:
        fib[1] = 1
    for i in range(2, max_n + 1):
        fib[i] = (fib[i-1] + fib[i-2]) % MOD
    
    T = int(input())
    for _ in range(T):
        N = int(input())
        if N < 2:
            print(0)
        else:
            total = 0
            # Sum over i from 0 to N-2
            for i in range(0, N - 1):
                # left part: length i, count = fib[i]
                # right part: length N-i-2, count = fib[N-i-2]
                right_len = N - i - 2
                if right_len >= 0:
                    term = (fib[i] * fib[right_len]) % MOD
                    total = (total + term) % MOD
            print(total)

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

A binary string of length N is a string of N characters, where each character is either 0 or 1. 

Wet Shark makes a list binStrings, consisting of all 2^N N-digit binary strings. For each string X in binStrings, Wet Shark runs a function ZeroShark(X), defined as follows (psuedocode):

bool ZeroShark(x):
//note the zero indexing
n = length(x)
counter = 0
FOR i = 1 to n-1
    IF x[i] == '0' and x[i - 1] == '0'
        INCREMENT counter
        END IF
ENDFOR
return (counter == 1)

Wet Shark stores an element X in a new list, binTrues  if ZeroShark(X) returns True for that X . In otherwords, binTrues consists of all the elements X in binStrings such that ZeroShark(X) returns True.

Given T testcases, where each testcase consists of integer N, calculate the length of binTrues for each N. Because the lists can get large, output your answer modulo 10^9+7=1000000007

INPUT

The first line of the input contains a single integer T, the number of such N.

The next T lines of the input contain a single integer N, as described in the problem.

OUTPUT

Output T separate lines, each containing the number of elements in the resulting list binTrues for each N in the input. The lists may be large, so output the answer modulo 10^9+7=1000000007

CONSTRAINTS

1 ≤ T ≤ 10

1 ≤ N ≤ 10^5

SAMPLE INPUT
2

2

3

SAMPLE OUTPUT
1

2

Therefore, binTrues is equal to ['00'], and has a length of 1.

Calling ZeroShark on each element in binStrings, the function only returns true when X is equal to 001 or 100. 

Therefore, binTrues is equal to ['001', '100'], and has a length of 2.

This means: the zeros must be isolated except for exactly one pair.

So the structure is: we have one "00" block, and all other zeros are isolated.

So the pattern is: 
- Exactly one occurrence of "00" (two consecutive zeros)

Actually, if we have a single '0' at position j ≠ i,i+1, it's fine. But if we have two '0's at j,j+1, that creates another "00" pair.

So the positions that can have '0' are:
- Only positions that don't form a consecutive pair with themselves

For the remaining positions (not i or i+1), each position can independently be '0' or '1', EXCEPT that we must ensure no two consecutive zeros.

This means the remaining positions form a sequence with no two consecutive zeros.

The number of ways to assign bits to a sequence of length k with no two consecutive zeros is Fibonacci-like.

For positions not at i or i+1, we have (N-2) positions to assign, but with the constraint that no two consecutive positions have both '0'.

But we also have the constraints from the "00" pair at i:
- If i > 0, position i-1 must be '1'
- If i+1 < N-1, position i+2 must be '1'

So for each i, the number of valid strings is:
- If i = 0: x[0]='0', x[1]='0', and for j ≥ 2, we need no "00" in the remaining positions
- If i = N-2: x[N-2]='0', x[N-1]='0', and for j ≤ N-3, we need no "00" in the remaining positions
- Otherwise: x[i]='0', x[i+1]='0', x[i-1]='1', x[i+2]='1', and for remaining positions no two consecutive zeros

Now implement with precomputation of Fibonacci numbers up to 10^5.

Since N ≤ 10^5, we need f(k) for k up to N-4 ≤ 10^5.

We'll precompute f[0] to f[100000] where f[0]=1, f[1]=2, and for i≥2: f[i] = f[i-1] + f[i-2].

Then for each query N:
- if N == 1: answer = 0
- if N == 2: answer = 1
- if N >= 3: answer = 2 * f[N-3] + (N-3) * f[N-4]

But when N=3: f[N-4]=f[-1], which is out of bounds.

So we define:
- f[0] = 1
- f[1] = 2
- for i >= 2: f[i] = f[i-1] + f[i-2]

And for N >= 3, we need f[N-3] and f[N-4].

For N=3: N-3=0, N-4=-1 → we need f[-1]

So define f[k] for k<0 as 0.

In code, when accessing f[n-4] for n=3, we access f[-1], so we need to handle negative indices.

We can do:
- if N-4 < 0: f(N-4) = 0
- else: f(N-4)

For N=2: we handle separately.

Final code:

```python
def solve():
    MOD = 1000000007
    
    # Precompute Fibonacci numbers up to 100000
    max_n = 100000
    f = [0] * (max_n + 5)  # f[i] is the i-th Fibonacci number with f[0]=1, f[1]=2
    if max_n >= 0:
        f[0] = 1
    if max_n >= 1:
        f[1] = 2
    for i in range(2, max_n + 1):
        f[i] = (f[i-1] + f[i-2]) % MOD
    
    T = int(input())
    for _ in range(T):
        N = int(input())
        if N == 1:
            print(0)
        elif N == 2:
            print(1)
        else:  # N >= 3
            # contribution from i=0 and i=N-2: each has f[N-3] ways
            # contribution from middle i: (N-3) * f[N-4] ways
            term1 = (2 * f[N-3]) % MOD
            if N - 4 < 0:
                term2 = 0
            else:
                term2 = (N - 3) * f[N-4]
            result = (term1 + term2) % MOD
            print(result)

solve()
```

Wait, for N=3: N-4 = -1 < 0 → term2=0, term1=2*f[0]=2*1=2 → total=2 ✓

For N=4: term1=2*f[1]=2*2=4, term2=(1)*f[0]=1 → total=5 ✓

Perfect.

But note: the Fibonacci sequence:
- f[0]=1 (0 positions: one way - empty)
- f[1]=2 (1 position: "0","1")
- f[2]=3 (2 positions: "01","10","11" → 3 ways)
- f[3]=5 (3 positions: 5 ways)

This matches.

Final code:

```python
MOD = 1000000007

# Precompute Fibonacci numbers up to 100000
max_n = 100000
f = [0] * (max_n + 5)  # f[i] is the i-th Fibonacci number with f[0]=1, f[1]=2
if max_n >= 0:
    f[0] = 1
if max_n >= 1:
    f[1] = 2
for i in range(2, max_n + 1):
    f[i] = (f[i-1] + f[i-2]) % MOD

T = int(input())
for _ in range(T):
    N = int(input())
    if N == 1:
        print(0)
    elif N == 2:
        print(1)
    else:  # N >= 3
        term1 = (2 * f[N-3]) % MOD
        term2 = 0
        if N - 4 >= 0:
            term2 = (N - 3) * f[N-4]
            term2 %= MOD
        result = (term1 + term2) % MOD
        print(result)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

A binary string of length N is a string of N characters, where each character is either 0 or 1. 

Wet Shark makes a list binStrings, consisting of all 2^N N-digit binary strings. For each string X in binStrings, Wet Shark runs a function ZeroShark(X), defined as follows (psuedocode):

bool ZeroShark(x):
//note the zero indexing
n = length(x)
counter = 0
FOR i = 1 to n-1
    IF x[i] == '0' and x[i - 1] == '0'
        INCREMENT counter
        END IF
ENDFOR
return (counter == 1)

Wet Shark stores an element X in a new list, binTrues  if ZeroShark(X) returns True for that X . In otherwords, binTrues consists of all the elements X in binStrings such that ZeroShark(X) returns True.

Given T testcases, where each testcase consists of integer N, calculate the length of binTrues for each N. Because the lists can get large, output your answer modulo 10^9+7=1000000007

INPUT

The first line of the input contains a single integer T, the number of such N.

The next T lines of the input contain a single integer N, as described in the problem.

OUTPUT

```python
    T = int(input())
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

A binary string of length N is a string of N characters, where each character is either 0 or 1. 

Wet Shark makes a list binStrings, consisting of all 2^N N-digit binary strings. For each string X in binStrings, Wet Shark runs a function ZeroShark(X), defined as follows (psuedocode):

bool ZeroShark(x):
//note the zero indexing
n = length(x)
counter = 0
FOR i = 1 to n-1
    IF x[i] == '0' and x[i - 1] == '0'
        INCREMENT counter
        END IF
ENDFOR
return (counter == 1)

Wet Shark stores an element X in a new list, binTrues  if ZeroShark(X) returns True for that X . In otherwords, binTrues consists of all the elements X in binStrings such that ZeroShark(X) returns True.

Given T testcases, where each testcase consists of integer N, calculate the length of binTrues for each N. Because the lists can get large, output your answer modulo 10^9+7=1000000007

INPUT

The first line of the input contains a single integer T, the number of such N.

The next T lines of the input contain a single integer N, as described in the problem.

OUTPUT

Output T separate lines, each containing the number of elements in the resulting list binTrues for each N in the input. The lists may be large, so output the answer modulo 10^9+7=1000000007

CONSTRAINTS

1 ≤ T ≤ 10

1 ≤ N ≤ 10^5

SAMPLE INPUT
2

2

3

SAMPLE OUTPUT
1

2

Explanation

When N=2, the list binStrings is equal to ['00', '01', '10', '11'].

Calling ZeroShark on each element in binStrings, the function only returns true when X is equal to 00. When X = 01, 10, 11, the counter in ZeroShark is equal to 0. 

Therefore, binTrues is equal to ['00'], and has a length of 1.

When N=3, the list binStrings is equal to ['000', '001', '010', '100', '011', '101', '110', '111'].

Calling ZeroShark on each element in binStrings, the function only returns true when X is equal to 001 or 100. 

Therefore, binTrues is equal to ['001', '100'], and has a length of 2.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have binary strings of length N
2. For each string X, we run ZeroShark(X) which counts the number of adjacent "00" pairs (where x[i] == '0' and x[i-1] == '0')
3. We want to count how many strings have exactly one such "00" pair
4. This count is the length of binTrues

## Analysis

Let me trace through the examples to understand the pattern:

**N=2:**
- '00': has one "00" pair at position (1,0) → counter = 1 → True
- '01': no "00" pairs → counter = 0 → False  
- '10': no "00" pairs → counter = 0 → False
- '11': no "00" pairs → counter = 0 → False
- Result: 1

**N=3:**
- '000': has "00" pairs at (1,0) and (2,1) → counter = 2 → False
- '001': has "00" pair at (1,0) → counter = 1 → True
- '010': no "00" pairs → counter = 0 → False
- '100': has "00" pair at (2,1) → counter = 1 → True
- '011': no "00" pairs → counter = 0 → False
- '101': no "00" pairs → counter = 0 → False
- '110': no "00" pairs → counter = 0 → False
- '111': no "00" pairs → counter = 0 → False
- Result: 2

## Pattern Recognition

For a string of length N, we need exactly one "00" pair in adjacent positions.

The "00" pair can occur at positions (i-1, i) for i from 1 to N-1.

For exactly one "00" pair:
- We have a contiguous "00" block of length at least 2
- The remaining positions must not contain any "00" pair

Let's think about valid configurations:

1. A single "00" pair followed by any valid configuration of the remaining positions
2. We need to avoid creating additional "00" pairs

Actually, let me reconsider: if we have exactly one "00" pair, it means there's exactly one position where we have two consecutive zeros.

The key insight: 
- If we have a "00" pair at position (i-1, i), then:
  - Position i-1 and i must be '0'
  - All other positions must not contain "00" pairs
  - This means we can't have another "00" pair

```python
def solve():
    MOD = 1000000007
    T = int(input())
    
    for _ in range(T):
        N = int(input())
        if N == 1:
            print(0)
        else:
            print((N - 1) % MOD)

solve()
```

def solve():
    MOD = 1000000007
    T = int(input())
    
    for _ in range(T):
        N = int(input())
        if N == 1:
            print(0)
        else:
            print((N - 1) % MOD)

solve()

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

A binary string of length N is a string of N characters, where each character is either 0 or 1. 

Wet Shark makes a list binStrings, consisting of all 2^N N-digit binary strings. For each string X in binStrings, Wet Shark runs a function ZeroShark(X), defined as follows (psuedocode):

bool ZeroShark(x):
//note the zero indexing
n = length(x)
counter = 0
FOR i = 1 to n-1
    IF x[i] == '0' and x[i - 1] == '0'
        INCREMENT counter
        END IF
ENDFOR
return (counter == 1)

Wet Shark stores an element X in a new list, binTrues  if ZeroShark(X) returns True for that X . In otherwords, binTrues consists of all the elements X in binStrings such that ZeroShark(X) returns True.

Given T testcases, where each testcase consists of integer N, calculate the length of binTrues for each N. Because the lists can get large, output your answer modulo 10^9+7=1000000007

INPUT

The first line of the input contains a single integer T, the number of such N.

The next T lines of the input contain a single integer N, as described in the problem.

OUTPUT

Output T separate lines, each containing the number of elements in the resulting list binTrues for each N in the input. The lists may be large, so output the answer modulo 10^9+7=1000000007

CONSTRAINTS

1 ≤ T ≤ 10

1 ≤ N ≤ 10^5

SAMPLE INPUT
2

2

3

SAMPLE OUTPUT
1

2

Explanation

When N=2, the list binStrings is equal to ['00', '01', '10', '11'].

Calling ZeroShark on each element in binStrings, the function only returns true when X is equal to 00. When X = 01, 10, 11, the counter in ZeroShark is equal to 0. 

Therefore, binTrues is equal to ['00'], and has a length of 1.

When N=3, the list binStrings is equal to ['000', '001', '010', '100', '011', '101', '110', '111'].

Calling ZeroShark on each element in binStrings, the function only returns true when X is equal to 001 or 100. 

Therefore, binTrues is equal to ['001', '100'], and has a length of 2.

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

Let me verify with N=4:

- i=3: x[2]='0', x[3]='0', x[1]='1' → "x100", x[0] can be 0 or 1 → "0100", "1100" → 2 strings
- i=2: x[1]='0', x[2]='0', x[0]='1', x[3]='1' → "1001" → 1 string
- Total: 2 + 1 + 2 = 5

Formula: 2^(4-2) + (4-3) * 2^(4-4) = 4 + 1 * 1 = 5 ✓

For N=3: formula should give 2
- For N=3: i=1, i=2 → each gives 1 string → total 2
- But our formula 2^(N-2) + (N-3)*2^(N-4) for N=3: 2^1 + 0 * 2^(-1) = 2 + 0 = 2 ✓

For N=2: formula gives 2^0 + (2-3)*2^(-2) = 1 + (-1)*1/4 = 0.75 → not valid.

So we need to handle N=2 separately, or adjust the formula.

Actually: for N=2, 2^(N-2) + (N-3)*2^(N-4) = 1 + (-1)*2^(-2) = 1 - 1/4 = 0.75 → wrong.

But when N=2:
- Only i=1: no restrictions on i>1 or i<N-1 (since N-1=1, so i<N-1 is false)

- i=1: 2^(N-3) strings
- i=N-1: 2^(N-3) strings

- If N = 1: no possible "00" pair (since need at least 2 positions) → 0
- If N = 2: 1
- If N ≥ 3: 2^(N-2) + (N-3) * 2^(N-4)

For N=2: we need to handle separately.

Actually, let's write it clearly:

- N = 1: 0 (no pairs possible)
- N = 2: 1

But for N=3: 2^(1) + 0 * 2^(-1) = 2 ✓

For N=4: 2^(2) + 1 * 2^(0) = 4 + 1 = 5 ✓

For N=5: 2^(3) + 2 * 2^(1) = 8 + 4 = 12

Let me verify manually for N=5:
- i=1: x[0]='0', x[1]='0', x[2]='1' → positions 3,4 free → 4 strings
- i=4: x[3]='0', x[4]='0', x[2]='1' → positions 0,1 free → 4 strings  
- i=2: x[1]='0', x[2]='0', x[0]='1', x[3]='1' → positions 4 free → 2 strings
- i=3: x[2]='0', x[3]='0', x[1]='1', x[4]='1' → positions 0 free → 2 strings
- Total: 4 + 4 + 2 + 2 = 12 ✓

Perfect!

So the solution:
- If N ≤ 1: 0
- If N = 2: 1  
- If N ≥ 3: 2^(N-2) + (N-3) * 2^(N-4)

We need to compute this modulo 10^9+7.

```python
def solve():
    MOD = 1000000007
    
    # Precompute powers of 2 up to 10^5
    max_n = 100000
    pow2 = [1] * (max_n + 1)
    for i in range(1, max_n + 1):
        pow2[i] = (pow2[i-1] * 2) % MOD
    
    t = int(input())
    for _ in range(t):
        n = int(input())
        
        if n <= 1:
            print(0)
        elif n == 2:
            print(1)
        else:  # n >= 3
            term1 = pow2[n-2]  # 2^(n-2)
            term2 = (n - 3) * pow2[n-4]  # (n-3) * 2^(n-4)
            result = (term1 + term2) % MOD
            print(result)

solve()
```

def solve():
    MOD = 1000000007
    
    # Precompute powers of 2 up to 10^5
    max_n = 100000
    pow2 = [1] * (max_n + 1)
    for i in range(1, max_n + 1):
        pow2[i] = (pow2[i-1] * 2) % MOD
    
    t = int(input())
    for _ in range(t):
        n = int(input())
        
        if n <= 1:
            print(0)
        elif n == 2:
            print(1)
        else:  # n >= 3
            term1 = pow2[n-2]  # 2^(n-2)
            term2 = (n - 3) * pow2[n-4]  # (n-3) * 2^(n-4)
            result = (term1 + term2) % MOD
            print(result)

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

A binary string of length N is a string of N characters, where each character is either 0 or 1. 

Wet Shark makes a list binStrings, consisting of all 2^N N-digit binary strings. For each string X in binStrings, Wet Shark runs a function ZeroShark(X), defined as follows (psuedocode):

bool ZeroShark(x):
//note the zero indexing
n = length(x)
counter = 0
FOR i = 1 to n-1
    IF x[i] == '0' and x[i - 1] == '0'
        INCREMENT counter
        END IF
ENDFOR
return (counter == 1)

Wet Shark stores an element X in a new list, binTrues  if ZeroShark(X) returns True for that X . In otherwords, binTrues consists of all the elements X in binStrings such that ZeroShark(X) returns True.

Given T testcases, where each testcase consists of integer N, calculate the length of binTrues for each N. Because the lists can get large, output your answer modulo 10^9+7=1000000007

INPUT

The first line of the input contains a single integer T, the number of such N.

The next T lines of the input contain a single integer N, as described in the problem.

OUTPUT

Output T separate lines, each containing the number of elements in the resulting list binTrues for each N in the input. The lists may be large, so output the answer modulo 10^9+7=1000000007

CONSTRAINTS

1 ≤ T ≤ 10

1 ≤ N ≤ 10^5

SAMPLE INPUT
2

2

3

SAMPLE OUTPUT
1

2

Explanation

When N=2, the list binStrings is equal to ['00', '01', '10', '11'].

Calling ZeroShark on each element in binStrings, the function only returns true when X is equal to 00. When X = 01, 10, 11, the counter in ZeroShark is equal to 0. 

Therefore, binTrues is equal to ['00'], and has a length of 1.

When N=3, the list binStrings is equal to ['000', '001', '010', '100', '011', '101', '110', '111'].

Calling ZeroShark on each element in binStrings, the function only returns true when X is equal to 001 or 100. 

Therefore, binTrues is equal to ['001', '100'], and has a length of 2.

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

A binary string of length N is a string of N characters, where each character is either 0 or 1. 

Wet Shark makes a list binStrings, consisting of all 2^N N-digit binary strings. For each string X in binStrings, Wet Shark runs a function ZeroShark(X), defined as follows (psuedocode):

bool ZeroShark(x):
//note the zero indexing
n = length(x)
counter = 0
FOR i = 1 to n-1
    IF x[i] == '0' and x[i - 1] == '0'
        INCREMENT counter
        END IF
ENDFOR
return (counter == 1)

Wet Shark stores an element X in a new list, binTrues  if ZeroShark(X) returns True for that X . In otherwords, binTrues consists of all the elements X in binStrings such that ZeroShark(X) returns True.

Given T testcases, where each testcase consists of integer N, calculate the length of binTrues for each N. Because the lists can get large, output your answer modulo 10^9+7=1000000007

INPUT

The first line of the input contains a single integer T, the number of such N.

The next T lines of the input contain a single integer N, as described in the problem.

OUTPUT

Output T separate lines, each containing the number of elements in the resulting list binTrues for each N in the input. The lists may be large, so output the answer modulo 10^9+7=1000000007

CONSTRAINTS

1 ≤ T ≤ 10

1 ≤ N ≤ 10^5

SAMPLE INPUT
2

2

3

SAMPLE OUTPUT
1

2

Explanation

When N=2, the list binStrings is equal to ['00', '01', '10', '11'].

Calling ZeroShark on each element in binStrings, the function only returns true when X is equal to 00. When X = 01, 10, 11, the counter in ZeroShark is equal to 0. 

Therefore, binTrues is equal to ['00'], and has a length of 1.

When N=3, the list binStrings is equal to ['000', '001', '010', '100', '011', '101', '110', '111'].

Calling ZeroShark on each element in binStrings, the function only returns true when X is equal to 001 or 100. 

Therefore, binTrues is equal to ['001', '100'], and has a length of 2.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have binary strings of length N
2. For each string X, we need to count the number of consecutive "00" pairs
3. ZeroShark(X) returns True if there's exactly ONE "00" pair in the string
4. We need to find how many strings have exactly one "00" pair

## Analysis

Let me trace through the examples:

**N=2:**
- "00": has one "00" at position (1,0) → counter = 1 → True
- "01": no "00" → counter = 0 → False  
- "10": no "00" → counter = 0 → False
- "11": no "00" → counter = 0 → False
- Answer: 1

**N=3:**
- "000": has "00" at (1,0) and (2,1) → counter = 2 → False
- "001": has "00" at (1,0) → counter = 1 → True
- "010": no "00" → counter = 0 → False
- "100": has "00" at (2,1) → counter = 1 → True  
- "011": no "00" → counter = 0 → False
- "101": no "00" → counter = 0 → False
- "110": no "00" → counter = 0 → False
- "111": no "00" → counter = 0 → False
- Answer: 2

## Pattern Recognition

For a string of length N, we need exactly one "00" pair. This means:
- We have exactly one occurrence where x[i] = '0' and x[i-1] = '0'
- This "00" pair can start at position 1 to N-1 (0-indexed)

Let me think about where the "00" pair can be:
- If the pair starts at position i (where i goes from 1 to N-1), then we have x[i] = '0' and x[i-1] = '0'
- All other positions must not create another "00" pair

For a "00" pair starting at position i:
- x[i-1] = '0', x[i] = '0'
- For positions j < i-1: if x[j] = '0', then x[j] and x[j+1] could form a "00" pair or not, depending on x[j+1]
- For positions j > i: if x[j] = '0', then x[j] and x[j+1] could form a "00" pair or not

- Exactly one occurrence where adjacent characters are both '0'

## Final Formula

For each N:
- If N < 2: answer = 0
- If N == 2: answer = 1
- If N >= 3:
